Found 2026-08-10, in the first successful interactive apply -- and it
undercuts that pass's headline result.

Apply wrote the reviewed span as
=CLOCK: [2026-08-10 Mon 12:46]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:47] =>  0:01=,
exactly as intended: =--review-apply-clock= deliberately calls *raw*
=org-clock-out= rather than =claude-code-ide-org-clock-out=, so the
5-minute rounding could not touch a human-confirmed interval. Committed
in that state (=14c42d2=). *Roughly five minutes later the line was
gone.* The next =Stop= hook ran =session-pause= ->
=claude-code-ide-org-clock-out= -> =consolidate-history=, which rewrites
the entire drawer -- including intervals written by something else
entirely.

*Confirmed by feeding =--consolidate-logbook-text= the exact committed
drawer text:*

: input:  CLOCK: [12:46]--[12:47] =>  0:01
:         CLOCK: [12:45]--[12:50] =>  0:05
: output: CLOCK: [12:45]--[12:50] =>  0:05      <- the 0:01 absorbed

and, more starkly, the same 0:01 interval *alone* with nothing to merge
into consolidates to the *empty string*. Any interval shorter than the
rounding granularity is not shortened or flagged; it is deleted.

*Why this is more than another short-interval bug.* The annotation
=- <12:46>--<12:47> interactive verification of review-and-apply=
survived, because it rides the =:other= bucket added in
[[id:ba8249c1-28cd-4ff1-918b-4b8439345d9a][ba8249c1]]. So the drawer now
holds an active-timestamp annotation describing an interval that no
longer exists anywhere in the record -- the annotation and the arithmetic
disagree, and only the annotation is right. A reader would trust the
CLOCK line.

Sibling of
[[id:293ac49e-3324-421c-8495-de1a46fc04ac][293ac49e]]
in spirit but not in mechanism: that one was org's own setting dropping
a zero-length clock at write time, and is fixed. This is *this module's*
rounding, destroying an already-written interval at an unrelated later
moment. Guarding the write was necessary and is not sufficient, because
nothing guards the record afterwards.

*Deliberately not repaired by hand yet.* Re-adding the 0:01 line now
would only feed it to the next clock-out -- the same reasoning
=ba8249c1= reached, where restoring a destroyed line before fixing the
destroyer was futile. Restore it as part of the fix, from =14c42d2=.

*Bearing on the cutover:* after
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] =session-pause= no
longer calls the consolidating wrapper, so the automatic trigger
disappears -- which is a reason the cutover is now more urgent, not a
reason to leave this alone. =consolidate-history= still has callers, and
an apply whose output survives only until something else touches the
drawer cannot be called correct.


*Fixed 2026-08-10.* Scope chosen by the user: retire rounding outright
rather than make it non-destructive, on the reasoning that it is worth
seeing how much of a problem runs of short intervals really are in
practice before building machinery to hide them.

Three changes:
- =--consolidate-logbook-text= no longer alters any interval. Rounding,
  merging and zero-dropping are gone; closed CLOCK lines are re-emitted
  with their endpoints exactly as recorded. It still orders newest-first
  and still preserves non-CLOCK lines, and =:SESSIONS:= day-collapsing is
  untouched -- this was about not rewriting *durations*, not about
  abandoning consolidation.
- =org_clock_out= no longer consolidates the heading it just closed.
  Rewriting a whole drawer as a side effect of closing one interval is a
  large blast radius for a cosmetic benefit, and it is the mechanism
  behind both this bug and =ba8249c1=.
- A *one-minute floor* on newly written intervals, since without rounding
  a same-minute clock in/out records =[12:46]--[12:46] =>  0:00=, which a
  clocktable sums as nothing at all. Implemented through =org-clock-out='s
  own =AT-TIME= argument, so org writes a correct line the first time --
  every previous attempt in this module to adjust an interval by editing
  drawer text afterwards has eventually destroyed something.

*The floor is a policy, not arithmetic,* and worth re-examining if it ever
grates: it invents up to fifty-nine seconds that did not elapse. Accepted
because a minute is already the resolution of a CLOCK timestamp -- there
is no honest way to write down less -- and because the alternative
misreports real short work as zero.

*=--round-time-to-5-minutes= and =--merge-time-intervals= are kept but
unwired,* with docstrings saying so. If the returning churn does prove
painful they are the raw material for a compaction pass a human invokes
deliberately, on history already reviewed. What must not come back is the
automatic rewrite on every clock-out.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 166/166. Three new tests (a bystander short
interval surviving an unrelated clock-out; the floor producing 0:01 and
never 0:00; the floor declining to touch an interval that already
exceeds it) and three existing ones inverted to assert preservation
where they used to assert rounding -- all six confirmed to fail against
the pre-change =config.el= by stashing it and re-running, not assumed.
Then live: =consolidate-history= re-run by hand on =720b2dcf='s real
drawer -- the exact function, on the exact data it ate -- reports
"Nothing to consolidate" and leaves the 0:01 interval intact.

*The destroyed interval is restored* from =14c42d2=, at its correct
newest-first position, so the annotation and the arithmetic agree again.

*Expected consequence, deliberately accepted:* per-turn clocking churn
returns to =:LOGBOOK:= until
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] stops per-turn
clocking altogether. That is the thing to watch.


** DONE Let the review buffer assign unattributed guideposts to a heading :code:

  spans reviewable; 14.6h recovered, 203 green
:END:

Guideposts carry no heading of their own; they inherit whichever one the
surrounding =clock_in=/=clock_out= bracket last named. Work done while no
clock is open therefore produces guideposts that belong to nothing, and
=--review-items-from-queue= drops them: it iterates the groups from
=--queue-events-by-id= under a =(when id ...)= guard, so the nil-keyed
group is skipped whole.

*Measured on this project, 2026-08-10.* A "what's next?" pass — reading
TODO.org, checking git state, running the suite, inspecting the queue,
forming a plan — ran from session start at 11:22 to the first =clock_in=
at 11:49. Twenty-eight minutes. The earliest CLOCK line written that day
is =11:50--12:00=, so none of it was recorded. Its two =resume=
guideposts (11:22:50, 11:31:02) are still in the queue and are not
merely unapplied but *unappliable*: with no bracket around them they can
never become a review item, so they will sit there indefinitely. That is
not a rare shape — planning, triage and backlog review are *by
definition* the moments when no task is clocked.

*The cutover does not fix this, and that is the point.*
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] leaves attribution
exactly where it is — the stream is still walked, the heading still taken
from queued =clock_in=/=clock_out= events, which is the stated reason
those two kinds are retained at all. What changes is *where the loss
happens*:

|             | today                        | after the cutover              |
|-------------+------------------------------+--------------------------------|
| loss is in  | the recorder — no CLOCK line | the reader — timestamps are    |
|             | is ever written              | on disk, nothing consumes them |
| recoverable | no                           | *yes, in principle*            |

So this becomes fixable without changing anything about what gets
written. The evidence is already durable; only the consumer throws it
away. =--queue-events-by-id='s own docstring already promises "an entry
keyed nil for events that could not be attributed to any heading" — the
data structure anticipates this case and the review layer simply
declines to use it.

*The proposal:* cluster the unattributed guideposts the same way
attributed ones are clustered, render the resulting spans in the review
buffer as unassigned, and let the human assign each to a heading at
review time — then apply it exactly like any other confirmed interval.

*Why this beats clocking into a standing bucket up front* (the
alternative recorded at
[[id:a1d63f52-4c07-4b9e-8f31-6d2b90c4e7a5][a1d63f52]]):
- It demands no discipline in the moment. Remembering to clock a bucket
  requires predicting that you are about to do meta-work, which is
  precisely when nobody is thinking about bookkeeping.
- It answers *about what*, not just *how much*. A bucket can total the
  meta-work but can never say which task you were circling.
- "Which task was that, actually?" is retrospective judgement a human
  can make and a machine cannot — the same premise the entire
  queue-and-review model rests on. Assigning at review time is that
  premise applied to attribution instead of to approval.

*Not a replacement for =a1d63f52=, and it does not close it.* A standing
heading stays useful as an assignment *target* for spans that genuinely
belong to no task — backlog-wide triage, review before any task exists.
It just stops being something you must clock into in advance, which is
the part that would not have survived contact with practice.

*Open, deliberately not decided here:*
- What a human does with a span they *cannot* place. Dropping it loses
  the honest answer "this happened and belonged to nothing"; leaving it
  pending re-proposes it at every future review, which trains people to
  ignore the buffer. Neither is obviously right.
- One session's nil-keyed group can hold several unrelated activities.
  The existing gap-threshold clustering splits them by time, which is a
  proxy for topic and will sometimes be wrong.
- Whether assignment should offer the same completion as =org-refile=,
  rather than a bespoke picker.


*Reframed 2026-08-11, after auditing the plans:* this is not a new idea.
=review-and-cutover.md='s Phase A rendering mockup already contains a
line reading =unattributed: 2 guideposts=, so displaying them was part of
the review command's design from the outset and was dropped somewhere
between plan and implementation without a note. Read this heading as a
*regression against an approved plan*, which raises its priority above
what a fresh proposal would carry — the design question was already
settled once.

*Measured 2026-08-13, and it makes this the highest-value item left
under the epic rather than a nicety.* Classified every queue event
against the real reader — =--queue-events-by-id= and
=--review-items-from-queue=, after reloading =config.el= — rather than
against a reimplementation:

| kind      | written | ever applied | coverage |
|-----------+---------+--------------+----------|
| =todo=      |      61 |           52 |   *85%* |
| =clock_in=  |      24 |            5 |      21% |
| =clock_out= |      25 |            2 |       8% |
| =pause=     |     159 |            9 |    5.7% |
| =resume=    |     163 |            9 |    5.5% |

*322 guideposts written; 18 ever consumed by an applied interval — 5.6%.*
The state-change half of the architecture works as designed. The
time-tracking half very nearly does not.

Of 351 currently-pending events, 340 are residue, in exactly two
buckets:
- *301 in the nil bucket* — 147 =pause=, 151 =resume=, 3 orphan
  =clock_out=. Unattributed, and this heading's subject.
- *39 attributed but unconsumed* — 19 =clock_in= + 20 =clock_out=, main-
  lane pairs on a heading that produced no interval because no turn
  boundary fell inside the window. Not this heading's problem; see
  [[id:78f485a8-e808-43ce-af5f-277a5c97c471][78f485a8]].

*Root cause, and it is structural rather than a bug.* A guidepost is
attributed only if a =clock_in= preceded it in the same lane
(=--queue-events-by-id= sets the lane's current heading on =clock_in=
and clears it on =clock_out=). Only 24 =clock_in= events exist across
four days, because Claude clocks in when it explicitly starts a tracked
task and not otherwise. So the overwhelming majority of turn boundaries
have no heading context and never can acquire one.

That is worth stating plainly against
[[id:720b2dcf-6af1-45f3-96a7-aa841e5651e1][720b2dcf]]'s core premise —
that the human reconstructs real =CLOCK:= intervals from guideposts. At
5.6% coverage there is very little to reconstruct *from*. This heading
is the mechanism that would make the other 94% reachable at all.

Note =--queue-events-by-id='s docstring already rejected using =todo=
events for attribution, on the sound argument that returning to a
heading which never left =DOING= emits no =todo= event, so later
guideposts would attribute to the wrong heading. That rejection assumed
=clock_in= would carry the load. Measured, it does not — which is an
argument for revisiting the rejected option alongside this one, not for
overturning it.

*Decided 2026-08-13: build it.* The deciding figures, measured against
the live queue after retuning the threshold to 1200s:

| what                                        | amount           |
|---------------------------------------------+------------------|
| CLOCK time recorded in org, 08-10..08-13    | *3.6 h* (23 intervals) |
| unattributed span time sitting in the queue | *14.6 h* (23 spans)    |

So the org record currently captures roughly *one fifth* of actual work
time, and the missing four fifths is already durable on disk with no
consumer. That is this heading's own prediction — "the loss moves from
the recorder to the reader ... recoverable, in principle" — now measured
rather than argued.

*The workload objection dissolved on contact with the numbers.* The fear
was that assignment means triaging hundreds of guideposts. It does not:
316 unattributed guideposts aggregate to *23 spans*, the same order as
the 23 intervals already reviewed. Review volume roughly doubles; it does
not explode. Two of the 23 are zero-width and write no =CLOCK:= line.

*Chosen UX: propose, human confirms.* Each unassigned span renders with a
*suggested* heading, derived from the nearest preceding =todo= event —
in practice whatever last went =DOING=. =RET= accepts, =a= assigns a
different heading, =d= dismisses it as not work time.

*This partly overturns a documented rejection, and the distinction is the
whole justification, so it must not be lost.*
=--queue-events-by-id='s docstring rejects deriving attribution from
=todo= events, because a return to a heading that never left =DOING=
emits no =todo= event and every later guidepost would then attribute to
the wrong heading. That objection is about *silent* attribution, and it
still stands: nothing here changes what =--queue-events-by-id= does. A
*suggestion the human confirms* has a different failure mode entirely —
a wrong suggestion is visible at the moment of decision and one keystroke
from correction, which is precisely the propose-then-confirm shape the
whole architecture rests on. Blank-by-default was considered and rejected
as 23 lookups where 23 confirmations will do.

*Deliberately out of scope for v1:* splitting one span across two
headings. A 90-minute span covering two tasks must currently be assigned
whole or dismissed. Worth revisiting only if it bites — the existing
=e= (edit interval) adjusts endpoints but does not split.

*Shipped 2026-08-13* (commits =3c6b3f2=, =9398e6a=).
=--review-items-from-queue= gained a second pass over the nil-keyed
group: its guideposts cluster into spans exactly like attributed ones
and carry a *suggested* heading, so accepting one is an ordinary =m= and
apply needed no new code path at all — =:id= is already the heading the
interval lands on. New: =--review-suggest-heading=,
=--review-heading-title=, =--assignable-files=,
=--heading-activity-range=, =claude-code-ide-org-review-assign= on =a=,
an =:unassigned= flag rendered =?= (or =!= when there is nothing to
suggest), and a =--review-markable-p= guard refusing a span with no
heading rather than letting apply fail later with a confusing error.

*Grew beyond the decision, at the user's prompting mid-implementation.*
The agreed scope was "propose, human confirms". Asked whether assign was
smart enough to exclude headings created after a span ended, it was not:
it offered all 110 IDs =org-id= knows about, including
=clock-template.org= fixtures and a =probe-state.org= scratch file left
in =/tmp= by that hour's debugging. Now 55, restricted to the tracked
files plus each one's archive target, with headings created after the
span excluded outright. The user then asked for each candidate's
recorded activity window, which answers "was this live then?" far better
than a title does — so the prompt shows =clocked 08-10-08-11= or =made
08-06=.

Worth recording that the *symmetric* case is not symmetric in strength:
a heading created after the span is impossible, but one closed before it
is merely implausible — documenting a task after marking it =DONE= is
routine — so that one demotes in the ordering rather than vanishing.

*Verified, not just implemented:* eight new tests, 195 -> 203, and every
one confirmed to fail against the unfixed code by stashing =config.el=
and re-running rather than by assumption. Two initially passed
*vacuously* — =seq-find= returns nil when no spans exist, so their
assertions never ran — and were strengthened to assert the span exists
first. That is the same worthless-test shape flagged earlier the same
day on
[[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][7387f97c]]; catching it twice
in one day is the argument for the stash-and-rerun check being routine
rather than occasional.

*Five defects found, none of them by the test suite.* Two by running it
against the live queue, three by reading real output:
1. Only 2 of 23 spans got a suggestion. Suggestions read the *pending*
   stream, but 85% of =todo= events are already applied — the watermark
   had eaten precisely the confirmed signal. =--queue-events= gained an
   =include-consumed= argument, used only for this question about
   history and never for building items. 2 -> 18 of 23.
2. The guess *latched*: three spans across eight hours all suggested a
   heading that had gone =CANCELLED= before the earliest of them began.
   The walk now releases the active heading when it leaves
   =DOING=/=PLANNING=.
3. =(?:...)= is PCRE; Emacs needs =\\(?:...\\)=. The end timestamp never
   matched, so every heading read as never-clocked.
4. =[^]]+= captured CLOCK lines quoted as *prose examples* in heading
   bodies, and =--parse-org-timestamp= rejects those by *signalling*, so
   one documentation line aborted the scan for a whole heading.
5. =--parse-org-timestamp= runs =string-match= internally, clobbering
   the match data — so reading group 2 after parsing group 1 returned
   the parser's match. Every interval collapsed to its start time, which
   looks like a plausible zero-length clock rather than a bug.

*First real apply, 2026-08-13.* Five items — two spans and three states
across two headings. Everything landed, and it doubled as the first live
confirmation of
[[id:6b1e73c4-25da-4f0e-8a51-9c0d3f7ab214][6b1e73c4]]: a chain recorded
=from: TODO= twice against an unmoved file produced
=State "NEXT" from "TODO"= then =State "DOING" from "NEXT"=, org deriving
the second from reality exactly as that heading argued it would.

Two findings spun out of that apply rather than being fixed here:
[[id:c97b3564-5b14-4fc5-8964-a64775c5dc5d][c97b3564]] (an assigned span
writes a label-less annotation) and
[[id:12e0adac-5aa4-4e95-a5d5-574ce32c1db0][12e0adac]] (the rationale
forbidding consolidation at apply is stale).

*What it was worth.* 14.6 hours of previously unreachable time became
reviewable against 3.6 hours then recorded. 23 spans, not 316
guideposts — the workload objection that nearly sank this heading
dissolved once measured.

CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 15:56]--[2026-08-13 Thu 15:57] =>  0:01

- [2026-08-13 Thu 15:56]--[2026-08-13 Thu 15:57] drained definition and archive command
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-13 Thu 15:57] \\
  seven files archived; 3.6h to 20.9h recorded
:END:

Proposed by the user 2026-08-11 as the middle ground between treating a
drained queue file as a permanent record and treating it as a spool:
mark batches consumed, but *guarantee ingestion is idempotent*, so an
old file can be un-marked and reprocessed — after a logic change, a
corruption, or a bug found later. An archive folder rather than a flag
was suggested as the more attractive mechanism.

*The archive half already exists, by accident.* It was created
2026-08-10 to hold the stranded =d847eab1= file, and it needs no code at
all: =--queue-session-ids= calls =directory-files= non-recursively,
filtered to =\.jsonl\'=, so a subdirectory is already invisible to the
reader. That is exactly why an archive folder beats a consumed-flag —
the flag would need reader changes, and this is proven working. What is
missing is a command to move drained files there (today a manual =mv=)
and one to bring them back.

*The idempotent half is one third done, and was not framed as such.*
Apply has three write paths:
- *State transitions — already effectively idempotent,* via
  [[id:f9f61c04-150b-4ee7-96c9-582cf2bda95a][f9f61c04]]'s staleness
  guard. Replaying an applied transition means its =from= no longer
  matches the heading's current state, so it is flagged and refused.
  That guard was built to prevent regressing a heading; it turns out to
  be the first piece of idempotent ingestion.
- *=CLOCK:= intervals — not idempotent.* =--review-apply-clock= drives
  =org-clock-in=/=org-clock-out= with no check on what is already there,
  so a replay writes a duplicate interval and silently doubles the
  recorded time.
- *Annotations — not idempotent.* =--append-to-drawer= checks only that
  the *drawer* exists, never that the line does.

*Proposal: content-based idempotency, not bookkeeping.* Both remaining
cases are cheap because the output is deterministic from the event. An
interval with identical endpoints on the same heading is a duplicate,
not two real intervals a human happened to confirm twice; an annotation
line is a pure function of its span and label, so a string match
suffices. Checking the destination is strictly better than tracking
applied identities in a sidecar, because it survives losing the sidecar
— which is precisely the situation reprocessing an archived file puts
you in.

*Archive the =.applied= sidecar alongside its =.jsonl=.* Otherwise a
restored file loses the record of what was already applied, and
selective reprocessing becomes impossible. Note the two reprocessing
modes want different things: fixing a corruption wants the sidecar
honoured, while re-deriving under new logic wants it ignored so
everything is re-examined, with content-idempotency absorbing the
overlap. Support both, or pick one deliberately.

*Growth is not a real objection.* These are short JSONL lines; a year of
heavy use is a few megabytes. The reason to bound the live queue
directory is review-buffer legibility, not disk.

*This resolves* [[id:9d2fcdad-9bf7-47b6-8018-223b13ec4577][9d2fcdad]]:
once archived files are permanent by default and restorable, the raw
pause/resume history has a durable home, and the =:SESSIONS:= drawer has
no remaining job.

*Define "drained" as "yields no items," not "all events applied" —
otherwise the move command has no trigger condition.* Added 2026-08-12
after measuring it, because the obvious reading is unreachable. Only
events that become *items* are ever marked applied, and two whole kinds
never do: =clock_in=/=clock_out= exist to attribute guideposts to a
heading and are consumed by nothing, and =pause=/=resume= that never
cluster into an attributable span produce no item either. Measured with
the review buffer reporting "Nothing pending": =todo= 31 of 31
consumed — every state transition ever queued — alongside 14 =clock_in=,
17 =clock_out=, 109 =pause= and 113 =resume= residual. 253 events, no
proposals. A file in that state is finished in every sense that matters
and would never be moved under an all-applied test.

Two consequences for this task's design. *The predicate is a property of
the reader, not of the sidecar* — "would =--review-items-from-queue=
build anything from this file" — so it belongs beside the item builder
rather than in the watermark. And *the =.applied= sidecar is not a
progress bar*: it will always look radically incomplete against the line
count, so any UI or check that reports "N of M applied" would read as
alarming when it is in fact the steady state.

*The residue is now classified, 2026-08-13, which is what this heading
needed before "drained" could be specified.* Of 351 pending events, 340
are residue and fall in exactly two buckets — see the table on
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] for the full
coverage figures:

1. *301 unattributed guideposts* (plus 3 orphan =clock_out=), owned by
   =3d0487f4=. This is 88% of the residue. Whether these are *ever*
   consumable is that heading's decision, and it therefore determines
   what fraction of the queue "drained" can possibly cover.
2. *39 attributed-but-unconsumed clock events* — 19 =clock_in= + 20
   =clock_out= — main-lane pairs on a real heading that produced no
   interval, because no =pause=/=resume= fell inside the window. Found
   2026-08-13 when a two-minute span recorded nothing. *This bucket has
   no owner.* It is not =3d0487f4='s (they are attributed) and not a
   defect (intervals come from guideposts by design), but it grows
   during ordinary use, so a definition of drained has to name it
   explicitly rather than treat it as noise.

Consequence for sequencing: this heading was *not* actionable until
=3d0487f4= was decided, because 88% of what it must classify as drained
or not is exactly that heading's subject.

*Unblocked 2026-08-13:* =3d0487f4= was decided — build it, with spans
rendered as suggestions the human confirms. That settles what happens to
bucket 1: those 301 events become *consumable*, so "drained" can mean
what it sounds like for them rather than having to carve out a permanent
exception. Bucket 2 (the 39 attributed-but-unconsumed clock events)
still has no owner and still needs naming explicitly in any definition of
drained — it is unaffected by that decision.

Note the ordering constraint that remains: specifying drained before
=3d0487f4= *ships* would still be premature, since its dismissal path
("=d= — not work time") determines whether an unassigned span leaves the
queue by being applied or by being dismissed, and those are different
terminal states for the same event. The asymmetry noted above (19
=clock_in= vs 20 =clock_out=, 3 orphans in the nil bucket) is also
unexplained and predates subagent activity — worth a look before
specifying, since a definition of drained that assumes pairs balance
would be wrong today.

*Asymmetry explained 2026-08-13, and it has two unrelated causes.*
Walked every session's stream pairing per lane, the way
=--queue-events-by-id= does:

1. *Five =clock_out= events with no open =clock_in=*, all in session
   =2d057349=, all pre-cutover. Their notes say what they were — "stray
   resume, not work on this heading", "closing out the review command".
   Pre-cutover the clock was a single live global thing, so a session
   could legitimately close a clock a *different* session had opened.
   The event records that correctly; there is simply no matching
   =clock_in= in the same file. Historical, and cannot recur now that
   =org_clock_out= opens and closes nothing.
2. *Four =clock_in= events never closed* — =720b2dcf=, =02aaae22=,
   =f290161c=, =feba67eb= — plus one legitimately open right now for
   this heading's own work. *This is not an anomaly, it is the normal
   end-of-session state.* Post-cutover the =Stop= hook appends a =pause=
   guidepost, not a =clock_out=; nothing ever closes a queued
   =clock_in=. A session that clocks in and ends leaves one dangling
   permanently.

So a definition of drained that assumes pairs balance would indeed be
wrong, and would stay wrong: unmatched events on both sides are a
*permanent* feature of a finished file, not a transient state it grows
out of. Both belong in bucket 2.

*A constraint this turned up that the design above does not mention, and
it is the sharpest one.* =bin/hooks/queue-append= appends with =>>=,
which recreates a missing file. So archiving the file of a session that
is *still writing* does not stop it writing — it splits that session's
stream across an archived file the reader no longer scans and a fresh
one it does. Nothing errors; the older half simply stops being
consulted mid-session.

Which means the drained predicate needs a second clause beyond "yields
no items": *the session must not still be live.* A currently-open
=clock_in= is one signal, but a weak one — most sessions never clock in
at all. The reliable one is the running session's own id, which the
review command knows; file mtime is a serviceable fallback for the rest.
Getting this wrong is worse than never archiving, because the loss is
silent and lands mid-session.

*Shipped 2026-08-13* (commits =31ad02d=, =8e9a678=, =be5834d=), all
three halves, idempotency first — it is the only one whose absence
damages silently, and the risk begins at the *first* archive run rather
than at some later reprocessing.

*Idempotency is content-based, as proposed.*
=--logbook-has-interval-p= refuses a =CLOCK:= line whose endpoints
already appear on the heading; =--drawer-contains-line-p= refuses a
duplicate annotation. Checking the destination beats a sidecar of
applied identities precisely because it survives *losing* the sidecar,
which is exactly what reprocessing does.

*The drained predicate has two clauses,* and the second was not in the
original design. =--queue-file-drained-p= requires that the file yield
no review items *and* be idle. The idle clause exists because
=bin/hooks/queue-append= appends with =>>=, which recreates a missing
file: archiving a still-writing session does not stop it writing, it
splits the stream across an archived file the reader no longer scans and
a fresh one it does. Nothing errors. =claude-code-ide-org-queue-idle-seconds=
defaults to an hour, deliberately generous — archiving late costs
nothing, archiving early costs a split stream.

*Archiving needed no reader change at all*, as this heading predicted:
=--queue-session-ids= reads non-recursively, so the subdirectory was
already invisible. =archive-drained-queues= takes a prefix argument for
a dry run; =restore-queue= takes one to leave the watermark behind so
every event is re-offered.

*Verified, not just implemented:* 203 -> 211 tests, each new one
confirmed to fail against unfixed code by stashing =config.el=. One is a
deliberate guard against the idempotency check *over*-matching and
passes both ways by design, stated so it does not resemble the vacuous
shape found twice this week.

Exercised for real rather than in a dry run only: seven session files
archived across three passes with their watermarks, and a restore round
trip checked end to end — the reader went 3 -> 4 sessions with the
watermark back in place, then 4 -> 3 on re-archiving.

*Two defects found by cross-checking output rather than by tests.*
1. =archive-drained-queues= called =nreverse= *twice* on the same list,
   once for the message and once for the return value. =nreverse= is
   destructive, so the second call operated on what had become the tail:
   the message printed four sessions correctly while the returned
   =:moved= held one. Human-facing output right, programmatic answer
   wrong. Caught only because =:moved= and =:skipped= stopped summing to
   the number of files on disk; the regression test now asserts that
   partition exactly.
2. =(substring id 0 8)= signals on anything shorter, at what turned out
   to be *five* sites including the render path. Real ids are UUIDs so it
   never bit in use. Replaced with =--short-id=: display code should
   degrade, not signal.

*The interaction this heading predicted, confirmed.* It warned that
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] would determine
what fraction of the queue "drained" could cover. It made it far
stricter: since a lone guidepost now clusters into a zero-width
unassigned span, and that *is* an item, a file drains only once a human
has assigned or dismissed everything in it. Two test fixtures written
against the old behaviour failed and were corrected, not the assertion.

*Spun out rather than fixed here:*
[[id:ffe65444-50ed-49f8-a4c3-27c1c4c843c3][ffe65444]] — narrowing a span
consumed the events it excluded — found by losing 32 minutes to it and
since fixed, and the recovery used exactly the property this heading
rests on: the =.jsonl= is append-only, so un-marking three timestamps
brought the interval back.

*What it was worth, measured.* Clock time recorded for 2026-08-10..13
went from *3.6 hours* before spans existed to *20.9 hours* across 53
intervals. The live queue holds one file — the session still running —
and seven are archived, restorable, and no longer in anyone's way.

- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-12 Wed 16:09] \\
  review-buffer interaction fixes
- [2026-08-12 Wed 16:09]--[2026-08-12 Wed 16:13] mark advance, note editing, bulk marks
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 16:13] \\
  mark advance, note editing, bulk marks shipped
:END:

Requested by the user 2026-08-11, after applying a real batch by hand —
so these come from use, not from imagining use. Deliberately sequenced
after [[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]]: polishing
the interaction before the queue's contents are trustworthy would be
tuning the wrong thing, since pre-cutover every =todo= item is stale by
construction.

1. *Mark and toggle advance to the next line.* The single biggest
   difference between marking ten items and marking ten items pleasantly.
   Standard everywhere in Emacs — =dired-mark=, =package-menu-mark-*=,
   =magit= — and its absence is why marking currently feels like typing
   =m= then =n= forever. Note =u= conventionally advances too, while a
   plain toggle arguably should not; follow dired, which advances on both.
2. *Allow editing the note.* =e= already edits an interval's endpoints;
   the note is the other half of what lands in the file, and it is the
   part a human is best placed to improve — Claude's 3-to-10-word note
   was written before the work was done, and by review time the human
   knows what it actually was. It becomes the =\\\\= continuation on the
   State line, so this is the last chance to fix it before it is history.
3. *An idiomatic mark-all / unmark-all / toggle-all.* Again dired's
   vocabulary: =m=/=u= per line, =U= to unmark everything, =t= to invert.
   Note =t= is currently bound to per-line toggle, so this is a rebinding
   and not just an addition — worth doing at the same time as (1) rather
   than teaching two different sets of muscle memory.

Related but distinct: [[id:118373e5-b89b-4001-ad52-1f68374745fb][118373e5]]
proposes a transient menu once there are filters worth composing. These
three are the keystroke-level fixes underneath that, and are worth
having whether or not a menu ever appears.

*Outcome, 2026-08-12.* All three shipped, with
[[id:e3f70e61-6616-4c57-81e8-e350ffd5824c][e3f70e61]] in the same batch
since both touch the renderer.

1. *=m= and =u= advance*, via a new optional ADVANCE argument to
   =--review-set-mark=. Advancing lands on the next *item* line
   specifically — never a blank or group heading — and stays put at the
   end of the list rather than running off it, since either would leave
   point somewhere the next keystroke reports "No review item on this
   line."
2. *=N= edits the note*, seeded with the current one. =e= keeps the
   interval, so the two halves of what reaches the file have a key each.
3. *=t= inverts every mark; =M= marks all, =U= unmarks all.* The
   per-line toggle binding was *removed* rather than moved: dired has no
   per-line toggle either, and does not need one once =m=/=u= advance.

*One design decision not in the original three.* Bulk marking *skips*
stale, unconfirmed items and reports the count, rather than prompting
per item. A =y-or-n-p= storm would be worse than offering no bulk
command — the same prompt-fatigue argument
[[id:6b1e73c4-25da-4f0e-8a51-9c0d3f7ab214][6b1e73c4]] used. Unmarking is
never refused, since it asks nothing of anyone.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 178 -> 183, five new tests — advance lands on an
item and does not overrun the end, unmark advances too, bulk marking
skips stale until confirmed then includes it, =N= changes what actually
reaches the *file* rather than only the buffer, plus e3f70e61's two.
Live-reloaded and the real queue rendered: two-line header, new bindings
resolving. Note =claude-code-ide-org-review-toggle= remains =fboundp= in
the running image — =load-file= does not unbind a deleted function — but
it is gone from source and nothing binds it.


** DONE A read-only target buffer costs the human their whole review pass :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 16:09]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8f2c1a90-4b7e-4d1a-9c3e-1f5a6b8d2e47
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 13:10]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 16:09] \\
  shipped; by-hand pass closed its last gap
CLOCK: [2026-08-11 Tue 13:14]--[2026-08-11 Tue 13:29] =>  0:15
CLOCK: [2026-08-11 Tue 13:11]--[2026-08-11 Tue 13:13] =>  0:02
:END:

Reported by the user 2026-08-11. Pressing =x= while =TODO.org= is
read-only did not fail in the cheap way: none of the =org-clock-in= /
=org-todo= writes could touch the buffer, so *every* item failed
individually with =buffer-read-only= — and =claude-code-ide-org-review-
apply= then refreshed unconditionally, rebuilding from the queue and
discarding every mark, every stale confirmation, and every corrected
interval. The decisions only ever existed in the review buffer, so a
failure that changed nothing on disk still destroyed all of the human's
work. That is exactly backwards for a design whose whole premise is
that the human's judgement is the expensive, irreplaceable step.

The user toggles read-only (=C-x C-q=) to guard =TODO.org= against
their own stray keystrokes while reading it, not to block this command
— so being *asked* is the whole fix.

*Fixed 2026-08-11, in two parts.*

1. *Pre-flight, not per-item failure.*
   =claude-code-ide-org--review-read-only-buffers= resolves each marked
   item's =:ID:= exactly the way apply does and returns the distinct
   read-only buffers apply would have to write to;
   =--review-ensure-writable= offers to clear them
   (="TODO.org read-only.  Make it writable and apply? "=) and, on a
   no, signals a =user-error= *before* the first item is applied, so
   the review buffer is untouched. Deliberately a prompt rather than a
   binding of =inhibit-read-only=: none of this module's tools override
   read-only silently, and the point is to tell the human what is in
   the way, not to route around them.

2. *No refresh when nothing applied.* Refreshing discards marks, which
   is right after a successful apply — the applied items are consumed —
   and wrong after a run where nothing landed. It now re-renders
   instead (which re-evaluates staleness against the file as it now
   stands) and restores point. This is the general guard: the
   pre-flight covers read-only specifically, but any total failure now
   keeps the marks.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 168/168, four new tests — detection (including
that two items on one heading are one prompt, and that an unresolvable
=:ID:= contributes nothing), both answers to the prompt, marks +
=:stale-confirmed= surviving a decline with the file unchanged, and
marks surviving an all-failed run while a run that applied something
still refreshes. Live-reloaded into the running Emacs and exercised
against the real =TODO.org= buffer with =y-or-n-p= stubbed both ways:
it named =TODO.org=, refused with the buffer still read-only on no, and
cleared it on yes.

*Not claimed:* the by-hand pass — a human actually pressing =x= on a
read-only buffer and answering the live prompt. Everything above is
batch plus a stubbed =emacsclient= call.

*Known remaining gap, deliberately not fixed here.* A *partial* failure
still refreshes, so marks and edited intervals on the items that failed
are lost. Preserving those means re-attaching decisions to rebuilt
items across a refresh (matching on =:ID:= + type + timestamps), and an
edited interval has no counterpart in the queue to match against at
all. Worth doing with
[[id:d409390b-6624-4820-82bb-730d763962fd][d409390b]]'s interaction
work rather than bolted onto this fix.

*Not-claimed gap now closed.* The by-hand pass this heading declined to
claim — a human pressing =x= on a read-only buffer and answering the
live prompt — happened for real on 2026-08-12, during
[[id:f290161c-f9e7-4bfb-9adf-4c5630d4c5ae][f290161c]]'s dismissal round
and the apply that followed it. The pre-flight named =TODO.org=, cleared
it on a yes, and the apply landed. Everything this heading built is now
exercised by a person, not a stub.

:END:

- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-11 Tue 22:29] \\
  implementing chain-aware staleness
- [2026-08-11 Tue 22:29]--[2026-08-11 Tue 22:37] implement projected-state stale check
- [2026-08-11 Tue 22:37]--[2026-08-11 Tue 22:37] implement projected-state stale check
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 09:58]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:02] =>  0:04
- [2026-08-12 Wed 09:58]--[2026-08-12 Wed 09:58] implement projected-state stale check
- [2026-08-12 Wed 09:58]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:02] implement projected-state stale check
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02] \\
  projected-state stale check shipped and verified
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/hazy-moseying-sky.md][Plan]]

Raised by the user 2026-08-11, from the right observation: *every* state
item in two consecutive real batches rendered =STALE=. That much is a
pre-cutover artifact and needs no fix — =org_set_todo= still applies the
change live *and* queues an event whose =from= is the state held before
that change, so
[[id:f9f61c04-150b-4ee7-96c9-582cf2bda95a][f9f61c04]]'s check compares
=from= against a heading that has already moved to =to=. It cannot
match. [[id:d409390b-6624-4820-82bb-730d763962fd][d409390b]] already
records this as "pre-cutover every =todo= item is stale by
construction."

*The part that does not end at the cutover.* Post-cutover nothing moves
the file until apply moves it. So a batch holding a chain on one heading
(=TODO -> NEXT=, then =NEXT -> DOING=) records the same =from= on both
events — whatever the unmoved file said. Apply the first and the file
moves; the second then re-checks its =from= against a file that has
moved *because of this very batch*, and is refused. Today's "everything
is stale" returns as "every chain past the first step is stale."

*Why that matters more than the noise itself.* A flag that fires on
nearly everything trains the reader to confirm without reading — which
is the exact reasoning f9f61c04 already used when it decided *not* to
flag legacy =null=-=from= events. A guard that is always on is
indistinguishable from no guard, except that it costs a keystroke each
time.

*Two candidate answers, neither chosen:*
- Check a chained item against the batch's own *projected* state — what
  the earlier marked items in this batch will have left behind — rather
  than against the file. Keeps =from= meaning "reality at queue time",
  which is what makes it authoritative.
- Have the writer record =from= as the previous queued =to= for the same
  =:ID:=, so the chain is self-consistent. Cheaper, but =from= then
  means two different things depending on whether an earlier event
  exists, and it can no longer disagree with reality — which is the
  property that makes the check work at all.

*Observed for real 2026-08-11, in the second post-cutover review pass.*
Two chains were queued in one session —
[[id:0308e7d7-06ea-48fb-8579-6a5a43277674][0308e7d7]] and
[[id:9f3c35c7-0229-4cd6-908b-779267ec9b97][9f3c35c7]], each
=TODO -> DOING= then =TODO -> DONE=, both events recording =from: TODO=
because nothing moved the file between them. What actually happens is
more precise than "the second is flagged":

- *Nothing renders as =STALE=.* At draw time the file still said =TODO=,
  so both events matched reality and the buffer was clean. The
  divergence does not exist yet.
- *=--review-apply-item= re-checks at apply time and refuses*, returning
  =Error: refused stale TODO -> DONE ... (heading is now DOING); mark it
  again to confirm= and changing nothing. Four of six items applied, two
  refused.
- *The post-apply refresh then shows them as =STALE=*, because now they
  are. Marking again, confirming, and applying a second time lands them
  correctly.

So the cost is *one apply pass per step of the chain*, not a spurious
warning — a =TODO -> NEXT -> DOING -> DONE= chain would need three. And
the history that results is correct either way: apply passes only
=(plist-get item :to)= to =org-todo=, so the =from= written into
=:LOGBOOK:= is computed from the buffer, never from the queue's stale
value. The run produced =State "DOING" from "TODO"= then
=State "DONE" from "DOING"= on both headings.

*The real usability defect, which outranks the extra pass.* The review
buffer renders both kinds of stale with the same string, while the
correct action is opposite. In this same batch,
[[id:8f2c1a90-4b7e-4d1a-9c3e-1f5a6b8d2e47][8f2c1a90]] showed "STALE,
heading is now DOING" and had to be *skipped* — the transition already
happened live pre-cutover, and confirming would write a =DOING -> DOING=
no-op. =0308e7d7= showed "STALE, heading is now DOING" and had to be
*confirmed* — the file moved one item earlier in this very batch.
Identical presentation, opposite correct answers, and nothing in the UI
distinguishes them; the distinction survived only because it was worked
out in conversation. A reviewer without that context has no way to
choose correctly, which is worse than the flag firing too often.

*This is an argument for the projected-state option above*, beyond
tidiness: it makes chained items not stale at all, so a =STALE= flag
goes back to meaning exactly one thing — the file moved for reasons this
batch does not account for — which is always "look closer." The
ambiguity disappears rather than needing to be explained.

Decide it during
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]], while the write
path is already open, rather than discovering it in the first
post-cutover review pass.

*Outcome (2026-08-12, commit =2ce42fc=).* Took the first candidate — the
projected-state check — and rejected the writer-records-previous-=to=
alternative for the reason recorded above, plus one this heading had not
named: it would force =queue-append= to read its own output file,
breaking the blind-=O_APPEND= property the queue rests on.

=--review-projected-staleness= walks a batch tracking each =:ID:='s
pre-batch state and the state earlier items will leave behind; an item
is stale only when =from= matches *neither*.
=--review-state-stale-p= prefers that annotation and falls back to the
old file-only comparison when there is none, which is what keeps direct
callers and the pre-existing tests meaning what they meant.
=--review-apply= computes it fresh immediately before the loop rather
than inheriting it from render — that is what preserves the guarantee
that a heading changed *since the buffer was drawn* is still caught.
Render projects over *marked* items only, since an unmarked item will
not move anything.

*Evidence was stronger than this heading recorded.* Checked against the
live queue rather than taken from the note: =0308e7d7= and =9f3c35c7=
are two-link chains as described, but =47c027d2= was a *three*-link
chain (=TODO -> NEXT=, =TODO -> DOING=, =TODO -> DONE=) with all three
events carrying =from: TODO=. Its already-applied =:LOGBOOK:= also
confirms the payoff claim directly — it reads =NEXT from TODO= /
=DOING from NEXT= / =DONE from DOING=, because org derives each written
=from= from reality. So the chain always produced *correct history*; the
fix removes confirmation prompts and apply refusals, not a data defect.

*One asymmetry worth keeping in mind:* =org_set_todo= reads =from= off a
file that never moves, while the =ExitPlanMode= hook passes
=QUEUE_APPEND_FROM= explicitly and so records the chain's own previous
=to=. Both shapes are legitimate and both occur; accepting either is
required, not lenient.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 167 -> 170 at this commit, three new tests — a
three-link chain applying in one pass with the right derived =from= on
each line; a genuinely out-of-band heading still refused *inside* a
batch that a chain applies in; and a chain rendering clean both unmarked
and with its predecessor marked. Live-reloaded and exercised against the
real queue: with nothing marked =f290161c='s =PLANNING -> DOING= showed
=!=, and marking its predecessor cleared it.

*Worth knowing:* that flag with nothing marked is *pre-existing*
behavior, not introduced here — confirmed by running the old file-only
comparison against the same item, which also reports stale. Marking the
predecessor is the gesture that resolves it.

*Accepted consequence, deliberately not fixed.* Applying only the first
link and leaving the rest means the leftovers face a moved file with no
batch to account for it, and they are correctly stale then. That is a
real divergence at that point, not this bug returning.

*One test failure worth recording, because the cause was not the code.*
The refusal test first reported =:applied 0=. Moving the second heading
to WAIT left the subject the sole TODO of its sibling group, so
=claude-code-ide-org--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo= correctly promoted
it to NEXT behind the test's back. The test now carries a third sibling
and says why.


** DONE The review buffer has no way to dismiss an item you will never apply :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f290161c-f9e7-4bfb-9adf-4c5630d4c5ae
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 16:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02] \\
  dismissal shipped, three residents retired
- State "DOING"      from "PLANNING"   [2026-08-11 Tue 22:26]
- State "PLANNING"   from "TODO"       [2026-08-11 Tue 22:12] \\
  planning the dismissal path
- [2026-08-11 Tue 22:27]--[2026-08-11 Tue 22:28] plan review-buffer item dismissal
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/hazy-moseying-sky.md][Plan]]

The watermark advances only for *applied* items — deliberately, and
rightly: an item skipped this pass is usually one deferred, not one
refused. But the keymap offers =m=/=u=/=t=/=e=/=RET=/=x=/=g=/=?= and no
dismissal, so an item you will never apply has no exit. It reappears at
every review, forever.

*Not hypothetical as of 2026-08-11:* three permanent residents after the
first post-cutover pass —

- the =dead-beef= phantom clock
  ([[id:5e2a1c04-7b3f-4d21-9c88-2f6e0a91b7d3][5e2a1c04]]), which cannot
  ever apply because its =:ID:= does not resolve;
- two pre-cutover =STALE= state events
  (=8f2c1a90= =TODO -> DOING= 13:09, =feba67eb= =NEXT -> DOING= 13:29)
  whose transitions the tools already performed live. Confirming them
  would write =DOING -> DOING= no-ops, the shape behind
  [[id:3d93021d-1d8a-4657-bc5c-77734f91fb9d][3d93021d]].

Today the only ways out are hand-editing a file the design calls
append-only, or forging watermark entries. Both are worse than the
problem.

*Design question, not just a missing key.* Dismissal and deferral are
different intents and the watermark cannot currently tell them apart —
it records "applied," which is a third thing again. Options worth
weighing: a separate =.dismissed= set beside =.applied=; a =kind= the
reader skips; or a reason-carrying dismissal, since "already applied
live pre-cutover" and "this event should never have existed" want
different treatment at any later audit. The pre-cutover stales are also
self-limiting — they stop being generated at the cutover — which argues
against over-engineering for them specifically.

*Outcome (2026-08-12, commits =2ce42fc= and =0942282=).* Chose the
reason-carrying dismissal, stored as a =dismissed= map (timestamp ->
reason) *inside* the existing =.applied= file. Rejected a separate
=.dismissed= file — it doubles the read and write paths and the
atomicity surface for no gain, since both facts concern one session's
queue and are always read together — and rejected a =kind= the reader
skips, which would mean writing into the queue the design keeps
append-only. Deferral needs no new spelling: it is still simply not
marking.

The load-bearing piece is not the map but the *writer*.
=--queue-mark-applied= previously serialized only its own key, so once
=dismissed= existed in the file any apply would have erased every
dismissal — and that symptom reads as "dismissal is broken" rather than
"apply clobbered it." Both writers now go through one
=--queue-watermark-write= that takes both facts, making the clobber
impossible by construction rather than by remembering. Bound to =d=;
=--queue-events= skips dismissed alongside applied.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 170 -> 175, five new tests, the load-bearing one
being the clobber regression asserted in *both* orders. Then the real
end-to-end pass, by hand: all three permanent residents retired — the
=dead-beef= phantom span and the two pre-cutover =STALE= transitions —
across *two* different sessions' watermark files, which exercised the
per-session write path. Pending items went 4 -> 1, and =5063f096= kept
all 13 of its applied entries alongside its new dismissals, which was
the case most at risk.

*A defect in this feature, found and fixed in the same session
(=0942282=).* Routing through the shared writer with a possibly-empty
applied set produced ="applied":null= — a shape that could not arise
before, since the only previous writer was always called with at least
one entry. Harmless in process, because Elisp spells the empty list, the
empty object and JSON null all as nil and this reader collapses them; a
round-trip test therefore *cannot* catch it. It is not harmless outside:
=d.get("applied", [])= does not fall back when the key is present
holding null, which crashed a real inspection script against a real
watermark file during this session. Empty sets now serialize as =[]= and
={}=, asserted on the bytes rather than the round-trip, and the four
existing files were normalized in place with content verified identical
across the rewrite.

*Left behind, inert:* normalizing iterated every session with a queue
file and so created an empty =386e84cc-….applied= that had not existed.
It reads ={"applied":[],"dismissed":{}}=, which the reader treats
exactly as a missing file. Not cleaned up — the delete was declined.


** DONE Restore the read-only flag after review-apply clears it :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       45ec3ff6-cde1-42a6-a899-6b11982112cb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 10:00]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:24]--[2026-08-12 Wed 11:29] =>  0:05
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:33]--[2026-08-12 Wed 11:35] =>  0:02
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:45]--[2026-08-12 Wed 11:48] =>  0:03
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 11:51]--[2026-08-12 Wed 11:57] =>  0:06
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 12:14]--[2026-08-12 Wed 12:39] =>  0:25
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:06]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:07] =>  0:01
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:13]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:20] =>  0:07
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:29]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:33] =>  0:04
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02] \\
  read-only restored via unwind-protect
- [2026-08-12 Wed 10:02]--[2026-08-12 Wed 10:33]
- [2026-08-12 Wed 11:24]--[2026-08-12 Wed 12:39]
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, right after the first real dismissal
round. =claude-code-ide-org--review-ensure-writable= offers to clear
=buffer-read-only= on every buffer an apply needs to write, and nothing
puts it back. The user sets that flag to guard against their own stray
keystrokes, so a silently-disabled guard is exactly the cost described
in [[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]].

*Why clear-then-restore here, when c8a97d9d rejected it.* That heading
weighed clear-then-restore against binding =inhibit-read-only= for the
*MCP tool wrappers* and rejected it on two grounds: a failure window if
a tool errors mid-call, and races between concurrent sessions
interleaving clear/restore. Neither transfers to this path. Apply is
only ever reached from a genuinely interactive command and is never
invoked programmatically — the load-bearing constraint of the whole
queue design — so there is no second session to race. And the failure
window closes with =unwind-protect=, which is the mechanism that
objection did not consider. c8a97d9d's verdict still stands for the
wrappers; this is a different path with different properties, not a
reversal.

Restores only what this command actually cleared, so a buffer the user
had already made writable is left alone.

*Outcome (2026-08-12, commit =de1e7e6=).*
=--review-ensure-writable= now *returns* the buffers it cleared, and
=claude-code-ide-org-review-apply= restores them in an
=unwind-protect=. Returning them rather than re-deriving the list
afterwards is what keeps a buffer the user had already made writable
from being switched to read-only by this command — restoring means
putting back what was borrowed, not imposing a flag that was never
there. The clear happens *outside* the =unwind-protect=, since declining
signals and there is then nothing to put back.

*Verified:* =bin/test= 175 -> 177, two new tests — restored after a
successful apply, and restored after an error thrown mid-apply, which is
precisely the failure window
[[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]] named as its
objection. The second test also covers the never-read-only case, where
the buffer must be left writable and no prompt asked.

*Confirmed live on real data, not just in batch.* The user applied three
queued =DONE= transitions into =TODO.org= at 10:02 with the fix loaded.
That apply had to make the buffer writable to land, and =TODO.org= was
read-only again afterwards — with the user confirming they did not
re-set it by hand. That is the whole loop: borrowed, written, returned.


** DONE Two display defects in the review buffer's rendering of a failed item :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 16:13]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e3f70e61-6616-4c57-81e8-e350ffd5824c
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 16:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 16:13] \\
  one defect fixed, one was never real
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-12 Wed 16:13] \\
  batched with the interaction fixes
:END:

Both seen 2026-08-11, on the one =dead-beef= item that will ever exist
once [[id:5e2a1c04-7b3f-4d21-9c88-2f6e0a91b7d3][5e2a1c04]]'s fix ships.
Recording them because the reproducer is about to become
unconstructible by ordinary means.

The good news first: an unresolvable =:ID:= does not crash the render.
The error string is substituted where the heading title goes, which is
the right instinct. But:

1. *Elisp escaping leaks into the display.* The title line reads

   : Error: no org heading found with :ID: \"00000000-dead-beef-...\

   with literal backslash-quotes and a dangling trailing =\=. The error
   string is being inserted with its printed representation intact
   rather than as display text.

2. *The note renders twice* on the clock line — once inside the
   suggested-interval clause and again after it:

   : - <...15:12>--<...15:16> negative test, bogus id must not enqueue  (suggested)   negative test, bogus id must not enqueue

Neither is dangerous, and neither is worth much on its own. Worth fixing
together with whatever touches the renderer next, since a title slot
that can hold an arbitrary error string is a general case, not one tied
to this event: any =--at-id= failure at render time takes the same path.

*Outcome, 2026-08-12, batched with
[[id:d409390b-6624-4820-82bb-730d763962fd][d409390b]]. One of the two was
real; the other never happened.*

*(2) The double note was real and is fixed.*
=--review-format-annotation= already ends with the note, because the
note is part of the =:LOGBOOK:= line it produces, and
=--review-describe= appended it a second time. Dropped from the clock
branch.

*(1) The escaping leak is not reproducible, and there was never a path
for it.* =--at-id= builds its message with =format= and =%s=, and
=--review-render= substitutes it with =%s= — neither prints an escaped
representation. Reconstructed the exact case (an item on an
unresolvable =:ID:=, rendered into a review buffer) and read the raw
buffer text: =Error: no org heading found with :ID:
"00000000-dead-beef-0000-000000000000"=, real quotes, no backslash
anywhere. *The =\"= and dangling =\= in the report above came from how
it was transcribed into this heading*, not from the renderer.

Worth stating plainly rather than quietly dropping: this heading asked
for a fix to something that was working. The reproducer was described as
about to become unconstructible, which is exactly why it was recorded —
and also why it was never checked before being written down. Both
behaviors are now pinned by tests, including one asserting the rendered
title contains no backslash, so a future =%S= cannot introduce the
defect this heading believed it had found.


** DONE Queue attribution is session-scoped, so concurrent subagents lose intervals :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-13 Thu 09:46]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0d789b68-faca-4911-868a-fdd70acbfd40
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 17:45]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 17:46] \\
  reproducible attribution bug, fix is small
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-12 Wed 22:39] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][Apply writes headless state notes -- RETRACTED, a grep artifact]] becoming NEXT.
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-13 Thu 09:43] \\
  re-promoted after auto-demotion side effect
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 09:44]--[2026-08-13 Thu 09:46] =>  0:02
- State "DOING"      from "NEXT"       [2026-08-13 Thu 09:44] \\
  implementing per-agent attribution lanes
- [2026-08-13 Thu 09:44]--[2026-08-13 Thu 09:46] fixing queue attribution
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-13 Thu 09:46] \\
  per-lane attribution; both agents pair correctly
:END:

Found 2026-08-12 by deliberately running two subagents concurrently to
exercise =agent_id=, which until that moment had never been populated
in 347 events. Both halves of the result matter: the field works, and
the code that consumes it is defeated upstream.

=--queue-events-by-id= resolves which heading an id-less event belongs
to by walking each *session's* stream with a single =current= variable
(config.el:2503-2510). Subagents share their parent's =session_id=, so
two concurrent agents interleave into one walk and clobber each other's
=current=. =org_clock_out= sends no =id= at all (config.el:359), so it
depends entirely on that variable.

Measured, not reasoned:

: 17:40:01  clock_in  e51d6ba1  agent=a8a8  -> current = e51d6ba1
: 17:40:20  clock_in  7771fc63  agent=ae39  -> current = 7771fc63  (clobbers)
: 17:42:20  clock_out  id=nil   agent=ae39  -> resolves 7771fc63, current = nil
: 17:46:  clock_out  id=nil   agent=a8a8  -> current already nil -> NIL BUCKET

=--review-items-from-queue= skips the nil bucket outright (=(when id
...)=, config.el:2515), so in a *single* review round =a8a8='s
=clock_out= is dropped and its =clock_in= is left orphaned in
=e51d6ba1='s group with nothing to close it: =e51d6ba1= yields *0*
clock items, =7771fc63= yields 2.

*Correction, 2026-08-12, from the apply pass that followed.* An
earlier draft of this heading stated the interval was lost outright.
It was not. Both intervals landed correctly, because the human applied
in *two rounds*: round one consumed =ae39='s pair, and in round two
=ae39='s =clock_in= was no longer in the pending stream to clobber
=current=, so =a8a8='s id-less =clock_out= re-attributed to
=e51d6ba1= and produced =17:40--17:43=. Reproduced deterministically by
replaying the real =--review-items-from-queue= against a copy of the
queue with =ae39='s events marked applied.

That makes the defect *worse*, not milder, and reframes it: *heading
attribution of id-less events depends on watermark state.* The same
queue file yields different attribution depending on what was applied
before it — so the record a heading ends up with is a function of when
the human happened to run apply and how they batched it, not of what
the sessions actually did. A one-round apply loses the interval; a
two-round apply recovers it by luck. Neither is a property anyone
chose.

*The near miss is worse than the loss.* =ae39= paired correctly purely
because the agents completed LIFO. Reverse the finish order and
=ae39='s interval attributes to =e51d6ba1= — a real duration on the
wrong heading, which is the confidently-wrong record
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] exists to
prevent. Silent loss is the benign branch of this bug.

The irony: the *pairing* logic immediately below is fully agent-aware
(=by-agent=, config.el:2531-2554, "pair each agent's own
clock_in/clock_out"), and human spans correctly exclude agent events
(config.el:2562). Only the attribution walk that runs first is blind to
=agent_id=.

Likely fix: key =current= by =(session_id . agent_id)= rather than
=session_id= alone, with nil =agent_id= as the main session's own lane.
Cheap, and the failing case is now reproducible on demand — spawn two
concurrent subagents that each bracket their work with
=org_clock_in=/=org_clock_out=.

Note the nil bucket already held two other unattributed =clock_out=
events predating this experiment, and =b74e0f19= and =9d2fcdad= each
show a =clock_out= with no =clock_in=. Whether those share this root
cause or a different one is unexamined.

*Fixed 2026-08-13.* =--queue-events-by-id= now tracks the current
heading per *lane* rather than per session: =agent_id= when the event
came from a subagent, the main session otherwise. A =clock_out= with no
=id= resolves against its own lane, so interleaving cannot mis-pair it
regardless of completion order.

A second defect fell out of the same change, unplanned. Guideposts
carry no =agent_id= — the =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= hooks fire in the
parent's turn — so under the old code a subagent's =clock_in= captured
every following human =pause=/=resume=. Real evidence: =7771fc63='s
group contained a stray =[pause]= and produced a spurious zero-width
=:suggested:= span. Guideposts now stay in the main lane, where they
belong.

*Verified, not just implemented:*

- Two regression tests, both of which *fail* against pre-fix code
  (checked by reverting =config.el= and re-running, not assumed).
  =queue-attributes-concurrent-agents-separately= runs the measured
  interleaving in *both* completion orders — LIFO was what accidentally
  saved one agent, so testing only that order would have proved
  nothing. =queue-guideposts-ignore-subagent-lanes= pins the guidepost
  half. 193 -> 195 green.
- Replayed against the *real* captured queue at its pre-round
  watermark, in the live Emacs after =load-file=. That exact input
  previously yielded one interval; it now yields both, correctly
  attributed, *in a single pass* — so the two-round recovery this
  heading documented is no longer needed to get the right answer:

: e51d6ba1 start=17:40:01 end=17:43:58 agent=a8a8 suggested=nil
: 7771fc63 start=17:40:20 end=17:42:20 agent=ae39 suggested=nil

Not addressed here: the pre-existing unattributed =clock_out= events
noted above. They predate subagent activity, so they have a different
cause and are still unexamined.

*Residue found and cleaned 2026-08-14.* The fix stopped new occurrences
but left behind the two it had already produced. Each of the two
subagent runs this project has ever queued carried, beside its correct
inactive annotation, a *zero-width active* one repeating the subagent's
own label:

: CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 17:40]--[2026-08-12 Wed 17:42] =>  0:02
: - [2026-08-12 Wed 17:40]--[2026-08-12 Wed 17:42] subagent investigating recovery fate
: - <2026-08-12 Wed 17:40>--<2026-08-12 Wed 17:40> subagent investigating recovery fate

Read off the queue: the main lane's own =pause= (17:40:26) and =resume=
(17:43:14) fell *inside* the two subagent intervals, were attributed to
the subagents' headings by the session-scoped logic this heading fixed,
clustered as lone guideposts into zero-width spans, and inherited the
enclosing =clock_in='s note as their label. Human spans always render
*active*, so unattended machine work reached =org-agenda= — which the
design explicitly forbids. The third lines were deleted from
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] and
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]]; the CLOCK lines
and inactive annotations are correct and were kept.

*Zero width is not the diagnostic, and this matters for anyone tempted
to sweep for it.* Seven other zero-width active annotations remain in
TODO.org and are all correct: a lone guidepost is one interaction point,
and recording it without a CLOCK line is deliberate. What marked these
two was a *subagent's* label on a *subagent's* heading.

*Found by eye, not by any check,* while planning a manual pass over the
agenda. Nothing detects this class of thing: a fix that stops new bad
data does not clean up old, and no test asserts over the contents of
:LOGBOOK: drawers in the real file — only over fixtures.
