- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-12 Wed 22:39] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][Apply writes headless state notes -- RETRACTED, a grep artifact]] becoming NEXT.
- [2026-08-12 Wed 19:00]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:24]
- [2026-08-13 Thu 09:43]--[2026-08-13 Thu 10:15]
:END:

Found 2026-08-12 while checking whether
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s "apply writes
already-closed intervals" premise holds. It holds for a *crash* — no
=save-buffer= runs between the two calls — but not for an *error*.

=--review-apply-clock= (config.el:2741-2751) calls =org-clock-in= then
=org-clock-out= with no =unwind-protect= between them; the
=unwind-protect= at config.el:3457 guards the read-only flag only. If
=org-clock-out= signals after =org-clock-in= succeeded, =--at-id='s
=condition-case= (config.el:63) swallows it into an error string and
that item's own =save-buffer= is skipped — but the clock stays running
and the buffer stays modified. =--review-apply= then *continues the
loop* (config.el:2896), and the next item on the same file calls
=save-buffer= (config.el:2858), committing the open =CLOCK:= line to
disk. A stray =C-x C-s= by the user does the same.

*Corrected 2026-08-12 by forcing the failure and reading the result
off disk.* The mechanism above is right; the symptom stated was wrong,
and the real one is worse.

*No open =CLOCK:= line ever reaches disk.* What happens instead: item
A's =org-clock-out= fails, leaving the clock running; the *next* clock
item's =--review-apply-clock= hits its defensive =(when (org-clocking-p)
(org-clock-out nil t))= (config.el:2732), which closes A's clock at
*now*. Measured, with an intended 0:30 interval:

: CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 10:00]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:56] =>  9:56

The fabricated duration scales with the distance between the backdated
start and wall-clock time — and writing backdated intervals is the
entire purpose of apply, so in practice this inflates by hours, not
minutes.

Two aggravating details, both measured rather than predicted:

- Apply returns =(:applied 1 :errors ("Error: ..."))=. The human sees
  one clean failure and *no* signal that the failed heading gained an
  interval it never earned.
- The heading gets no annotation line at all, because
  =--append-to-drawer= (config.el:2752) sits after the clock block and
  is skipped by the same error. So the fabricated =CLOCK:= line looks
  exactly like a legitimate one, with nothing marking it suspect — the
  confidently-wrong record
  [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] exists to
  prevent.

Reproduction, described rather than linked because it lived in an
ephemeral session scratchpad: build a temp org file with two headings,
stub =org-clock-out= via =cl-letf= to signal on its *first* call only,
pass two clock items on the two headings to
=claude-code-ide-org--review-apply= in one batch, then read the file
back *from disk* rather than from the buffer — reading the buffer would
not have shown what was committed. Cheap to rebuild; a proper
regression test belongs in =config-test.el= as part of the fix.

*Fixed 2026-08-12.* An =unwind-protect= around the clock pair
(config.el:2741-2751); on a non-local exit it calls =org-clock-cancel=
rather than retrying the close. Cancelling is the honest cleanup: the
item is reported as an error, so its events are never marked applied
and the interval stays pending for a later pass. No record beats a
confident wrong one, and retrying a call that just signalled is not a
recovery strategy. The cancel is wrapped in =ignore-errors= so a
failure there cannot mask the original error, which is the one the
human needs to see.

*Verified, not just implemented:* a new regression test
(=review-failed-clock-out-writes-no-interval=) stubs =org-clock-out= to
signal on its first call, applies two clock items in one batch, and
asserts *exactly one* =CLOCK:= line reaches disk — the surviving item's,
at its exact endpoints. It *fails* against pre-fix code and passes
after, which is the property the three tests written for
[[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][7387f97c]] conspicuously
lacked. 192 -> 193 green. Re-running the original live-Emacs probe after
=load-file= confirms the same end to end: the failed heading now carries
no drawer at all, and =org-clock-out= is called twice rather than three
times, because the next item's defensive close no longer finds a clock
running.

*Still unestablished, and it gates how urgent this was rather than
whether the fix is right:* whether =org-clock-out=
can actually signal here with both endpoints supplied and
=org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= bound nil. The probe simulates
the failure rather than provoking a real one, so this demonstrates the
*consequence* of a premise that has not itself been shown reachable.
Reading =org-clock.el= for reachable signal paths is the remaining
step. If none exists, this is a latent trap rather than a live bug —
worth fixing cheaply either way, since an =unwind-protect= around the
pair costs one line.

Originally reached by two separate reviews of the apply path, both
reasoning from the same code. That is no longer offered as
corroboration: two readers making the same inference from one text is
not independent confirmation, as [[id:7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9][7387f97c]]
demonstrated the same day.

*Premise confirmed reachable, 2026-08-13 — this was a live bug, not
insurance.* The open question was whether =org-clock-out= can actually
signal on apply's path, with both endpoints supplied and
=org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= bound nil. Read =org-clock.el=:
it has exactly two signal sites, both bypassed when =fail-quietly= is
non-nil and apply passes nil:

- =(user-error "No active clock")= — guarded, since apply checks
  =org-clocking-p= immediately after its =org-clock-in=.
- =(error "Clock start time is gone")= (org-clock.el:1841) — raised when
  the line at =org-clock-marker= no longer looks like a =CLOCK:= line.

The second is *reachable, and observed*. It is the identical error
recorded as incident 1 of
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][53b0047d]], whose subject is
precisely a desynced =org-clock-marker=. So the chain is: a direct file
write desyncs the marker -> apply's =org-clock-out= signals -> without
the =unwind-protect= the clock stays open -> the next clock item's
defensive close writes a fabricated interval at *now*.

Consequence for sequencing: =53b0047d= is upstream of this heading and
should not be cancelled while this failure mode matters. Recorded there
too.

One further path worth noting, not fixed here:
=claude-code-ide-org--audit-clock-hook= runs on =org-clock-out-hook=
*unguarded*, and org runs that hook only after the =CLOCK:= line is
written (org-clock.el:~1898). A signal from it would leave a correct
interval plus a failed item — a different and milder shape than the one
fixed, but it would still mark the item failed while its write stood.


** CANCELLED Apply writes headless state notes -- RETRACTED, a grep artifact :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:45]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       7387f97c-6c3e-4667-8851-203af024fdd9
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 19:14]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 19:15] \\
  native state logging silently malformed
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:25]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:33] =>  0:08
- State "DOING"      from "NEXT"       [2026-08-12 Wed 19:25] \\
  diagnosing the missing State header
- [2026-08-12 Wed 19:25]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:33] fixing headless state notes
- [2026-08-12 Wed 19:33]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:42] fixing headless state notes
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:39]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:42] =>  0:03
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:42]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:45] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-12 Wed 19:42]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:45] fixing headless state notes
- State "CANCELLED"  from "DOING"      [2026-08-12 Wed 19:45] \\
  no defect; grep artifact, see body
- [2026-08-12 Wed 19:46]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:58]
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 19:56]--[2026-08-12 Wed 19:58] =>  0:02
CLOCK: [2026-08-12 Wed 22:36]--[2026-08-12 Wed 22:41] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-12 Wed 22:36]--[2026-08-12 Wed 22:41]
:END:

*Retracted 2026-08-12 the same day it was filed. There was no defect.*
Every transition the apply pass wrote carries org's full native header,
correctly formed:

: - State "CANCELLED"  from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-12 Wed 17:37] \\
:   :SESSIONS: deleted; ordering settled by af7d3687

All eleven applied transitions have one; =grep -c '^- State "'= counts
55 in the file.

*The defect was an artifact of the command used to look for it.* The
diff was filtered through =grep -v "^[+-][+-]"=, intended to drop the
=+++=/=---= file headers. That pattern also drops every *added* line
beginning with =-= — which is exactly the shape of an org log entry
(=+- State "CANCELLED" ...=). What survived the filter was the indented
continuation lines alone, which read convincingly as headless notes.

Kept rather than deleted because the investigation it triggered
produced things worth having, and because the failure mode is worth a
name:

- Three tests survive in =config-test.el= pinning apply's state-log
  format — with a note, without one, and with the trigger hooks firing.
  They assert correct behavior that nothing asserted before, which is a
  real gap this closed even though the bug was imaginary.
- Two probes established that apply writes correct headers in the
  *live* Doom session under every condition tried: read-only buffer
  cleared mid-flight by =--review-ensure-writable=, and a four-item
  batch containing a three-link chain. Both were written to test
  hypotheses about a defect that did not exist; both are reusable.
- CLAUDE.md's documented =#+TODO:= line is genuinely wrong — it omits
  the =(t!)= log markers the real files have carried since =a943f61=.
  Found while chasing this. Belongs to
  [[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]].

*The lesson, stated plainly because it recurred three times in one
sitting:* every wrong conclusion here came from trusting a derived view
— a filtered diff, a fixed-width =sed= window that crossed heading
boundaries, a scratch file built from documentation instead of the real
header — over the artifact itself. The file said the opposite each
time, and reading it directly took one command. Prefer the primary
artifact; treat any filter as a hypothesis about what it hides.


** DONE An assigned span writes a label-less annotation line :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:08]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c97b3564-5b14-4fc5-8964-a64775c5dc5d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 13:50]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-13 Thu 16:08] \\
  provenance labels; suppression would break agenda
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:00]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:01] =>  0:01
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:07]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:09] =>  0:02
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 13:51] \\
  label-less annotation from assigned spans
- [2026-08-13 Thu 15:57]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:12] assigned at review
:END:

Found 2026-08-13 in the first real apply of
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]]'s spans. Compare
what the two span paths wrote into the same drawer:

: - <2026-08-13 Thu 12:24>--<2026-08-13 Thu 13:09>
: - <2026-08-13 Thu 13:16>--<2026-08-13 Thu 13:22> building unassigned span assignment

The first is an *assigned* span, the second an ordinary bracketed one.
Unassigned spans are built with =:note nil= because there is no
enclosing =clock_in= to inherit a label from — correct as far as it
goes, but the resulting annotation restates the =CLOCK:= line directly
above it and adds nothing. =clock-template.org='s convention is that
every non-=CLOCK:= line carries a short label; a bare one is noise
against that standard.

Two candidate fixes, and the choice is a real one:
1. *Suppress the annotation entirely* when there is no note. Cheapest,
   and the =CLOCK:= line already carries the interval. Cost: the
   *active* timestamps disappear with it, and those are deliberate —
   they are what puts a human span into =org-agenda= (recorded on
   =--review-format-annotation=). Suppressing would silently remove a
   span from the agenda, which is a behaviour change disguised as
   tidying.
2. *Synthesise a label* — "assigned at review", or the heading the human
   picked, or the fact that it was a suggestion accepted rather than
   chosen. Keeps the agenda timestamps and says something true about
   provenance, which the bracketed path cannot say.

Leaning (2) on the agenda point, but it is worth checking whether a
label-less annotation actually reaches =org-agenda= before deciding —
if it does not, the argument for (1) is much stronger.

*Shipped 2026-08-13* (commit =8e9a678=), taking option 2 — synthesise a
label. The check that decided it: a label-less annotation still reaches
=org-agenda=, because the agenda matches the *timestamp*, not the text
around it. So suppressing the line would have quietly dropped the span
from the agenda — a behaviour change wearing the clothes of a tidy-up,
and the cheaper-looking option was the wrong one.

The synthesised label carries *provenance*, the one thing the timestamps
cannot say: this interval was reconstructed and confirmed, not clocked
as it happened. Two variants, because accepting a suggestion and
choosing a heading are different acts — =suggested span, accepted at
review= and =assigned at review=. A real note always wins over both.

Implementation note worth keeping: this needed a *permanent* =:origin=
tag alongside the transient =:unassigned=, which =a= clears. Without it
the annotation could not tell, after assignment, that the interval had
been reconstructed at all.

*Verified live, not just tested.* Before: 17 bare =- <ts>--<ts>= lines
in a single apply pass. After: every span written carries its label, and
the first real one read
=- <2026-08-10 Mon 12:36>--<2026-08-10 Mon 12:59> assigned at review=.
Fixed going forward only, at the user's instruction; the 17 already
written stand as they are.


** DONE Reconsider consolidating at apply: the rounding hazard that forbade it is retired :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:41]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       12e0adac-5aa4-4e95-a5d5-574ce32c1db0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 13:50]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 13:51] \\
  stale rationale; consolidation may be safe now
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:38]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:41] =>  0:03
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-13 Thu 16:38] \\
  deciding whether apply should consolidate
- [2026-08-13 Thu 16:38]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:41] consolidation at apply
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-13 Thu 16:41] \\
  apply consolidates its own heading's drawer
:END:

*A documented rationale has outlived its cause.* Apply deliberately does
not consolidate the drawer it writes into, and
=--aggregate-guideposts='s docstring gives the reason:

: Nothing here rounds: `claude-code-ide-org--consolidate-logbook-text's
: 5-minute rounding silently drops any interval under ~2.5 minutes that
: misses a boundary, which is precisely the confidently-wrong record
: this design exists to prevent.

That was true when written and is false now.
=--consolidate-logbook-text='s own docstring opens *"This function no
longer alters any interval"* — the rounding was retired outright in
[[id:b74e0f19-5a26-4c83-9d70-8e1c5a2f6b04][b74e0f19]], the incident
where it erased a real two-minute interval. So the hazard that kept
consolidation off the apply path no longer exists, and the comment
asserting it is stale in a way that would survive review indefinitely,
because it reads as a settled decision rather than a live claim.

*What this makes possible.* After the 2026-08-13 apply,
[[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]'s drawer reads:

: CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 12:24]--[2026-08-13 Thu 13:09] =>  0:45
: - State "TODO"       from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
:   ungated; gate was unsatisfiable and a proxy
: CLOCK: [2026-08-10 Mon 20:48]--[2026-08-10 Mon 20:49] =>  0:01
: - <2026-08-13 Thu 12:24>--<2026-08-13 Thu 13:09>

Four entries, four different positions on the timeline, in none of them.
[[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]] built exactly the
function that fixes this and proved it a byte-identical fixed point
against =clock-template.org=, but nothing calls it: it came off the
clock-out path and never gained another caller. Its own accepted cost
was that "a live drawer drifts back out of order between
consolidations" — and this is that drift, now visible in real data
rather than predicted.

*Why this is a decision and not a fix.* Consolidating at apply changes
what apply writes, and apply's whole claim is that it writes exactly
what the human confirmed and nothing else. Reordering lines the human
did not ask to reorder is a small violation of that, even if the result
is more legible. Points to weigh:
- It is *idempotent* and provably lossless — =af7d3687= pins both, and a
  second pass is asserted to be a no-op.
- It touches lines the current apply never wrote, including history from
  other sessions, so a bug in it damages more than the batch.
- The alternative is a separate human-invoked command, which keeps apply
  minimal at the cost of another thing to remember.

Whatever is decided, the stale comment on =--aggregate-guideposts= must
be corrected either way — it currently misinforms anyone reasoning about
this.

*Decided and shipped 2026-08-13: yes, but scoped to the heading apply is
already writing.* Apply now normalises that heading's drawer between the
write and the =save-buffer=, so one apply remains one write.

The scoping is the whole decision. Apply's claim is that it writes what
the human confirmed and nothing else; tidying a drawer it is opening
anyway stretches that, while reordering drawers it never touched would
break it. A bug in consolidation now damages one heading's history —
which is also the one place a human is about to look, rather than
somewhere they will not notice for weeks.

Safe to do at all only because
[[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]] had already pinned
=--consolidate-logbook-text= as idempotent and provably lossless: a
byte-identical fixed point against =clock-template.org=, with a second
pass asserted to be a no-op. The engineering was done; only the call
site was ever in question.

=claude-code-ide-org-consolidate-on-apply= (default =t=) turns it off.

*Two stale statements corrected in the same pass*, both of which read as
settled fact:
- =--aggregate-guideposts='s claim that consolidation rounds. It has not
  since =b74e0f19=.
- =consolidate-history='s own docstring, which described re-emitting
  intervals "newest-first ... every non-CLOCK line after it" — the
  behaviour =af7d3687= replaced, still being described in the very
  function that replaced it.

*A note on how the tests got here*, because it is the more useful part.
Both new tests initially passed *without* the fix. org inserts each
=CLOCK:= line at the top of the drawer, so applying newer-then-older
comes out ascending by accident; only older-then-newer leaves it
inverted. The fixtures had it backwards and asserted a property that was
already true. Caught by stashing =config.el= and re-running — the check
that had been written into a memory minutes earlier, and which found its
first defect immediately.


** DONE Editing a span's interval does not re-scope its events, so truncating loses the remainder :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:08]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ffe65444-50ed-49f8-a4c3-27c1c4c843c3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 15:42]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:25]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:29] =>  0:04
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:32]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:33] =>  0:01
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:35]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:36] =>  0:01
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:38]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:41] =>  0:03
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:49]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:50] =>  0:01
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:54]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:59] =>  0:05
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 17:01]--[2026-08-13 Thu 17:08] =>  0:07
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 17:10]--[2026-08-13 Thu 17:16] =>  0:06
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 16:17]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:20] =>  0:03
- State "DONE"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-13 Thu 16:08] \\
  narrowing conserves events; e now splits spans
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 15:42] \\
  silent loss on truncate; recovery is available
- [2026-08-13 Thu 16:17]--[2026-08-13 Thu 16:20] suggested span, accepted at review
- [2026-08-13 Thu 16:25]--[2026-08-13 Thu 17:16] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:

*Found 2026-08-13 by losing 32 minutes of record to it*, acting on
Claude's own advice. Recorded with that provenance because the advice
was the proximate cause, not the code alone.

A 54-minute unassigned span (08-10 18:12--19:07) straddled two tasks.
The suggested remedy was to truncate it with =e= to the first task's
portion and let the remainder return on a later pass. Truncation worked
— =CLOCK: [18:12]--[18:35] =>  0:23= landed correctly — but the
remainder did not return. The guideposts at 18:32, 18:50, 18:51 and
19:07 are all marked *applied*.

*Mechanism.* =claude-code-ide-org-review-edit-interval= rewrites
=:start= and =:end= and deliberately clears =:suggested=, but never
touches =:events=. =--review-apply= then marks every event in that list
consumed via =--review-record-applied=. So an edited item writes the
*narrowed* interval while consuming the *original* span's events. The
narrower the edit, the more is silently swallowed.

*The claim that was wrong, stated plainly so it is not repeated:* "the
events you don't apply stay queued, so the tail isn't lost." That holds
for an item you *skip* — =--review-record-applied='s docstring says so
explicitly, and it is what makes applying a subset safe — but not for an
item you *edit and apply*. Skipping and narrowing are different
operations and only one of them is conservative.

*Nothing is destroyed*, which is the only reason this is a defect and
not an incident: the =.jsonl= is append-only and every event is still
there. Only the watermark asserts they are consumed. Recovery is
removing those timestamps from the =.applied= set, after which the
remainder is re-offered as its own span — and content idempotency
(=78f485a8=) makes re-offering safe, since the 18:12--18:35 interval
already on disk cannot be written twice.

*Candidate fixes, in rough order of appeal:*
1. *Re-scope =:events= when the interval is edited* — keep only those
   falling inside the new endpoints. Makes truncation mean what everyone
   assumed it meant, and the remainder genuinely returns. The obvious
   fix, and it makes =e= a real splitting tool.
2. *Refuse to narrow*, allowing only widening. Cheap and safe, but
   removes the one workaround for a span covering two tasks.
3. *Warn* that N events fall outside the new interval and will be
   consumed anyway. Honest, but leaves the sharp edge in place.

(1) also nearly delivers what
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] listed as
explicitly out of scope — splitting one span across two headings —
since truncate-assign-repeat becomes a working split once the remainder
survives.

*Shipped 2026-08-13* (commit =be5834d=), taking option 1 and adding the
user's refinement: the remainder appears *on the spot*, in the same
buffer, rather than at the next refresh.

=review-edit-interval= now partitions =:events= at the new endpoints,
keeps the ones the interval still spans, and hands the rest to
=--review-insert-remainders=, which clusters them at the usual gap
threshold and appends them as fresh items. A remainder inherits the
heading as a *suggestion* but reverts to unassigned when the original
was reconstructed — narrowing usually means the tail belongs elsewhere,
which is the whole reason the human reached for =e=. It never inherits
=:marked=: a remainder is a new decision, not part of the batch already
confirmed.

*Why on the spot matters*, and it was the user's point: the remainder is
the entire reason to narrow, so the human is mid-thought about which
task the tail belongs to. Deferring it to the next refresh is where it
gets abandoned.

*The lost 32 minutes were recovered first,* and the recovery is the best
evidence for the design this heading sits inside: the =.jsonl= is
append-only, so un-marking three timestamps in the =.applied= sidecar
brought the interval back as its own span (18:50--19:07), which was then
assigned and applied normally. Nothing had to be reconstructed by hand.

*Verified:* 211 tests, the new one confirmed to fail against the unfixed
code. It asserts the conservation property directly — every event of the
original span is still accounted for, in either the narrowed item or the
remainder.

*Consequence beyond the fix:* =e= is now a working span splitter —
truncate, assign, repeat — which
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] had listed as
explicitly out of scope for v1.


* DONE pause/resume cannot see a turn blocked on a permission prompt :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue

[[file:~/.claude/plans/cheerful-riding-robin.md][Plan]]

The =Stop= hook fires when a turn ends. A turn that stalls mid-flight —
waiting on a tool-permission prompt — has not ended, so no =pause= is
written and the queue records one unbroken span.

Measured in this session, 2026-08-11: the turn for prompt =523077c9= ran
=resume= 13:55:19 → =pause= 14:53:10, 57m51s. Of that, 54m11s was a
single tool call awaiting approval while the user was on a Zoom call;
roughly 3m40s was work. Nothing in the queue distinguishes the two.

Not a hook bug — the hook behaved correctly, the turn genuinely never
stopped. The guidepost model simply has no event for "still running, but
blocked on a human." Note the asymmetry with an *interrupted* turn,
which also emits no =pause= but is harmlessly bounded by the next
=resume=; this case is bounded by nothing.

Bears directly on the project's second stated goal. If a clock had been
running, ~58 minutes would have been credited to a heading for ~4
minutes of attention. Related:
[[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]], which is one
possible external signal for exactly this gap.

*Implemented 2026-08-14.* =PermissionRequest= opens a block and
=PostToolUse=/=PermissionDenied= close it, paired by =tool_use_id= at
review time; a block's timestamps are then removed from the guidepost
stream so the span splits around the wait. Design is in the linked plan;
what follows is what happened.

*The first fixture did not discriminate, and finding that out changed
what the feature is for.* The test reproduced the measured 2026-08-11
case — a 54m11s block — and it passed with the block logic disabled
entirely. A 54-minute hole already exceeds the 1200s gap threshold, so
=--aggregate-guideposts= splits it unaided. The honest consequence:
*for the case this heading was written about, the existing threshold was
already sufficient.* The machinery earns its keep only for blocks
*shorter* than the threshold — a 10-minute permission wait, which nothing
else can see. The fixture now uses exactly that, and a companion test
asserts the same guideposts without block events cluster into one span,
so neither half can pass without the other.

Worth stating because the heading's own framing — "the queue records one
unbroken span" — was right about the mechanism and wrong about that
measurement: at 1200s the queue would have split it. The threshold was
retuned to 1200s on 2026-08-13 (=f39944f=), *after* the 08-11
measurement, so the framing was true when written and quietly stopped
being true.

*One semantic bug, caught by the fixture rather than by review.* Blocks
were first excluded with endpoints *included*, which deletes the
=block_start= and =block_end= timestamps themselves — and those are the
last moment of work before the wait and the first moment after it. Every
neighbouring span collapsed to zero width. Exclusion is now strictly
interior, and the span-break test is non-strict on both sides, because
the timestamps either side of a block normally *are* its endpoints: no
guidepost fires while a turn is stalled, so a strict test never fires in
the case the feature exists for.

*Differs from the plan:* the plan had =PermissionRequest= call
=queue-append= directly and drop the sentinel inline. It got its own
script, =bin/hooks/block-start=, because =queue-append= knows nothing
about sentinels and teaching it would put ephemeral coordination state
inside the append-only writer. The pair is symmetric and each is
readable alone.

*Verified, in three layers, none of which is the one that matters most.*
=bin/block-hooks-test= (11 assertions, new) covers the sentinel round
trip, the cheap decline on an unblocked call, idempotency on replay,
refusal of a payload with no =tool_use_id=, and path traversal in both
ids. =bin/queue-append-test= gains 9 assertions, including that a block
does *not* inherit the blocked tool's =id= or =note= — the payload is
that tool's own, so a prompt on =org_set_todo= would otherwise attribute
the block to whatever heading that call named — and that an =Error:=
response does not suppress a =block_end=, since a denied call still ends
a real block. =bin/test= gains 6 ERT tests, each confirmed to fail
against the unfixed code.

*Not verified, and this is the load-bearing gap:* no =PermissionRequest=
has fired since the hooks were wired, so nothing confirms Claude Code
emits that event with a =tool_use_id= in this environment, or that it
does so inside a background job. Both are assumptions about the harness
rather than about this code, and neither can be exercised from a test —
they need one real permission prompt in a real session. Until that
happens the suites passing mean the plumbing is right, not that it is
connected. Hence =WAIT= rather than =DONE=.

*How to resume this, set up 2026-08-14 so the next person does not have
to work it out.* An =ask= rule forces a prompt even where the tool would
otherwise be allowed, so =.claude/settings.local.json= (gitignored,
untracked) carries =Bash(echo afk-probe:*)=. Run =echo afk-probe:= , leave
the prompt unanswered, approve on return, then:

: grep -h '"kind":"block' ~/.claude/org-updates/*.jsonl | jq -r '.ts,.kind,.tool_use_id'

Two lines sharing a =tool_use_id=, and =org_pending_updates= showing the
span split around the wait rather than through it.

*Pause for five to ten minutes, not longer, and the upper bound is the
whole point.* The gap threshold is 1200s; a wait longer than that is
split by the ordinary gap rule and the probe passes whether or not any of
this code exists — the exact trap the first fixture fell into. Only a
sub-threshold wait can distinguish the block events from the threshold
doing the work.

*Either failure is informative and belongs here rather than being
retried silently.* If no prompt appears, something in this session's
permission posture outranks =ask= — the 57 =allow= rules do not account
for the novel commands run on 2026-08-14, so the session is already
permissive somehow — and the finding is that the feature is invisible in
the configuration normally used. If the prompt appears but no
=block_start= is written, the =PermissionRequest= hook needs a fresh
session; hook-config changes are known to take effect live for
=PreToolUse=/=PostToolUse= (DONE.org =:ID:= 135033ff) and that has never
been tested for this event.

*Probe fired 2026-08-14 17:55, and it produced nothing.* The =ask= rule
was loaded and correct; =echo afk-probe:1= ran in 13 seconds with no
prompt, no =block_start=, and no sentinel. So the first failure mode
above is the one that happened, and the feature is currently invisible.

*Cause established immediately afterwards, and it is neither of the two
guesses above: the session was in auto mode.* Auto-accept approves
without prompting, so an =ask= rule never gets its chance. Recorded
because the two hypotheses reached from inside the session — background
job, or permission rules being read only at session start — were both
plausible, both wrong, and both would have cost a fresh session to
disprove. The user knew in one word what the evidence could not settle.

*So the probe is unchanged but its precondition is not:* leave auto mode
first (shift-tab cycles the permission modes), then run =echo
afk-probe:1=, wait five to ten minutes, approve. Nothing else about the
recipe changes.

*Second probe, manual mode, 2026-08-14 17:58:23--18:04:22. The prompt
appeared and was held for six minutes; the queue recorded none of it.*
That is failure mode #2 above, and it is worth two separate conclusions.

*The defect is now demonstrated live, on demand, rather than recalled.*
The queue for that window holds =resume= 17:57:07 and then nothing at all
until the turn ends — no =pause=, because the turn never ended, and no
=block_start=. Six minutes of waiting sit inside one unbroken run of
guideposts. The 2026-08-11 measurement was a 54m11s instance of the same
thing; this one is reproducible in a single command, which makes it a
regression fixture rather than an anecdote.

*And the fix did not fire, though it is correctly wired.*
=PermissionRequest= is present in =.claude/settings.json= — checked, not
assumed — and =bin/hooks/block-start= is executable and passes its own
harness. So the remaining question is narrowed to exactly one thing:
whether a session picks up a hook for an event type that was not
registered when it started. =135033ff= established that hook-config
changes take effect live, but only for =PreToolUse=/=PostToolUse=, both
of which were already wired at that session's start. Adding a new *event*
is a different claim and this is the first test of it.

*Next step, and it is the last unknown:* start a brand-new Claude Code
session in this repo, in manual mode, and run the probe again. If
=block_start= and =block_end= appear with a matching =tool_use_id=, the
feature works and the constraint is simply that new hook events need a
restart — worth adding to the org-dev skill, whose section 5 currently
says the opposite is true for hooks generally. If they still do not
appear, the fault is in the hook wiring rather than in session lifetime,
and =bin/block-hooks-test= passing means the fault is upstream of the
scripts.

*Worth noticing either way:* a permission block cannot occur in a session
that never prompts, so in background jobs this whole defect is
unreachable and the spans there were never wrong for this reason. That
narrows where the fix matters rather than diminishing it.

*Third probe, fresh session, 2026-08-14 18:24--18:41: the root cause is
now known, and it is neither session lifetime nor the hook wiring.*
=PermissionRequest= fires, =bin/hooks/block-start= runs, and it exits at
its own guard because *the payload carries no =tool_use_id=*. Captured
live at 18:40:51 by temporarily teeing the script's stdin — the keys are
=session_id=, =prompt_id=, =permission_mode=, =hook_event_name=,
=tool_name=, =tool_input=, =transcript_path=, =cwd=, =effort= and
=permission_suggestions=, and that list is complete. =PostToolUse=, by
contrast, *does* carry =tool_use_id=. The join key exists on the closing
event and not on the opening one, so the pair can never form.

*This falsifies the plan's central design assumption* — "=tool_use_id= is
the join key, and it makes the bracket exact." It is not available where
it is needed. The only keys present on *both* events are =session_id= and
=prompt_id=, and =prompt_id= is per *user prompt*, not per tool call, so
it cannot disambiguate two blocked calls within one turn. Re-keying the
pairing is real design work, not a patch, and it is what remains.

*The guard that hides it is tested as an edge case and is in fact the
production path.* =bin/block-hooks-test= asserts "block-start refuses a
payload with no =tool_use_id=" — written as path-traversal-adjacent
hardening, and passing throughout. Every layer of verification stayed
green while the feature was inert, which is exactly the failure the
heading's own "the plumbing is right, not that it is connected" warned
about; the warning was right and still did not catch it, because no
fixture ever took production's shape. =bin/queue-append-test='s header
says a fixture that never takes production's shape is a test of the
fixture. That is what happened here.

*The restart question is now unanswerable in retrospect, and no longer
matters.* This session started with =PermissionRequest= registered and
the hook demonstrably ran — but a hook that exits at the guard leaves
*exactly* the evidence a hook that never fired leaves: no queue line, no
sentinel. So the 17:58 session's silence has two explanations and the
observation cannot separate them. Consequently *nothing here justifies
correcting the org-dev skill's section 5* — the "new event types need a
restart" claim was never established, only conjectured, and the conjecture
is now moot.

*The defect itself reproduced a third time, sub-threshold.* A prompt held
18:32:53--18:38:38 (5m45s, against the 1200s gap rule) sits inside
=resume= 18:29:02 and nothing further — one unbroken run, no =pause=,
no =block_start=. The =touch= marker's own mtime of 18:38:38 is what
proves the call was held rather than merely slow.

*The recorded probe recipe does not work and should not be reused.* =echo
afk-probe:1= under the =ask= rule produced *no prompt* on three attempts,
sandboxed and unsandboxed; no competing =allow= rule matches it in any
layer and the global allow list is empty, so =echo= appears to bypass
permission evaluation entirely. =touch= on an unpermitted path prompts
reliably and is the better probe. Also worth recording because it was
believed otherwise at the time: the payload reports
=permission_mode= as =acceptEdits=, not a fully manual mode.

*Set =NEXT= 2026-08-14 rather than =WAIT=, deliberately.* =WAIT= would
mean waiting on upstream to add =tool_use_id= to the =PermissionRequest=
payload — a real dependency, but one with no filed request and no reason
to expect it. Nothing external is owed here; the remaining work is ours.

*Next action: re-key the pairing, and check first whether it needs a key
at all.* The hypothesis worth testing before any design: a permission
prompt blocks the turn, so prompts may be strictly serialised — never
more than one outstanding per session. If that holds, the sentinel does
not need naming per tool call. One slot per session suffices:
=block-start= sets it, and the next =PostToolUse= to arrive clears it,
whichever tool it belongs to, because the turn was stalled on precisely
that call. That is *less* machinery than the current
per-=tool_use_id= scheme, not more.

*Cross-session concurrency is already handled and is not the risk here* —
worth stating because it is the first objection raised, and it is aimed
one level out from where the exposure is. The sentinel path is
=$queue_dir/$session_id.blocked/$tool_use_id=, namespaced by session at
the *directory* level, and each session writes its own queue file. Two
sessions blocked at once cannot collide today and still cannot if the
per-tool-call layer collapses to a single slot. What would sink the
single-slot design is two permission requests outstanding *within one
session* — parallel tool calls in a single assistant block, both needing
approval. Rarer, but decisive, and it is the thing to probe. If it turns
out to be reachable, note that =prompt_id= does *not* rescue the design:
it is per user prompt, so both calls would carry the same one.

*Probed 2026-08-14 18:57--18:59: prompts are serialised, so the
single-slot design holds.* Two =Bash= calls were issued in one parallel
block, both requiring approval, with =PermissionRequest= timestamped by
a temporary tee in =block-start=:

: A  fired 18:57:01  ->  executed 18:58:47
: B  fired 18:58:47  ->  executed 18:58:57

*B's =PermissionRequest= did not fire until A had been approved and
run* — the same second. The permission layer serialises even calls the
model dispatched in parallel, so two prompts cannot be outstanding at
once and a block never has to be told apart from another block. One
sentinel per session is therefore sufficient, and no join key is needed:
=block-start= sets the slot, the next =PostToolUse= clears it, and the
absent =tool_use_id= stops being a problem instead of needing a
substitute.

*Corroborating detail, now measured rather than assumed:* both events
carried the same =prompt_id= (=219b7d7b=), confirming it is per user
prompt and not per tool call. That was the stated reason it could not
serve as a join key; it is moot under serialisation, but it is no longer
a guess. The payload also reported =permission_mode= as =default=,
where the 18:40 capture said =acceptEdits= — the mode does change under
foot, which is why the probe reads it from the payload rather than
trusting the session's belief about itself.

*Honest limit on the observation:* one trial, two =Bash= calls. It does
not establish that *every* pair of parallel tool calls serialises — a
mix of tool types, or an MCP tool alongside a builtin, is untested. The
single-slot design should therefore treat a second =block_start= arriving
while the slot is already set as a case to handle rather than an
impossibility; overwriting the slot and dropping the earlier open is the
conservative choice, since it loses one block rather than inventing one.

*Fixed and verified end to end 2026-08-14 (=c8c33a1=).* The sentinel
became one unkeyed slot per session and
=claude-code-ide-org--block-intervals= now pairs positionally over
ts-sorted events. Named =.block-open= rather than reusing =.blocked=
deliberately: a leftover directory from the keyed scheme would satisfy
=block-end='s =-e= test forever and close blocks that were never opened.
=queue-append= was not touched, so the queue format is unchanged — a
=block_start= simply carries a null =tool_use_id=, which is what the
event actually contains.

*The live probe, 19:24:38--19:30:30, which is the layer every previous
round was missing:*

: block_start  19:24:41  tool_use_id=null
: block_end    19:30:15  tool_use_id=toolu_017B5Yek...
: blocks       19:24:41--19:30:15 (334s)
: spans        18:22:52--19:24:41 | 19:30:15--19:30:18 | 19:30:30--19:30:30

The span breaks at the prompt and resumes at the approval. *The 334s
wait is below the 1200s gap threshold*, so the ordinary gap rule would
have clustered straight through it — the split is the block machinery
and nothing else, which is the discrimination the first fixture failed
to have. Note the payload asymmetry surviving into the record: null on
the open, a real id on the close.

*Discrimination proved before the fix was kept, not after.* Stashing the
implementation while retaining the new tests produced 9 failing shell
assertions and 4 failing ERT tests, =block-start accepts a payload with
no tool_use_id= among them. The fixtures were rebuilt from the payload
captured at 18:40:51 field for field, because the previous ones invented
a =tool_use_id= on =PermissionRequest= and every assertion passed
against the invented shape while the hook refused every real one.

*What the whole episode is actually about.* Three suites were green
across three sessions while the feature had never once fired. Not one of
them was wrong about the code; all of them were wrong about the input.
The heading's own warning — "the plumbing is right, not that it is
connected" — was written before any of this and did not prevent it,
because a warning in prose cannot fail a build. What eventually caught
it was teeing a real payload to disk, which is the cheapest step in the
entire sequence and the last one anybody tried.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on
2026-08-14,* with three siblings, on the reasoning that heading already
established twice: a defect in what the queue *records* does not have to
be fixed for the queue and its review loop to be complete and correct.
An epic that absorbs every defect found in the code it produced can
never close, and [[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]]
is waiting on this one closing.

Raised by the user 2026-08-12, on moving
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] to =DOING= because
some of its children had become active — which is the right thing to
express, and should happen whenever a child goes active. The problem is
what came with it: =claude-code-ide-org--trigger-auto-clock-in= fires on
*any* transition to =DOING=/=PLANNING=, hand-edits explicitly included,
so the toggle also opened a clock on the epic. That interval was
unwanted and was deleted by hand.

*Org already does the roll-up, natively.* Measured on this very epic
before the deletion — the clocktable read =14:52= for the parent row,
the ten children summed to =13:44=, and the =1:08= difference is exactly
the epic's own two CLOCK lines. So "activity rolls up to the parent in
reporting" needs no configuration; it is what =:maxlevel= does. The
consequence is the actual argument: a parent's own time is *additive,
inside the same total*, so it cannot be told apart from the children's
sum once rolled up. Clocking a container does not produce a roll-up — it
adds a second, differently-meaning quantity to one that already existed.

*The counter-precedent, and why it does not transfer.* norang's org-GTD
setup ([[https://doc.norang.ca/org-mode.html]]) deliberately puts time on
projects: clocking out of a subtask *cascades up* to the parent so
unattributable time lands somewhere. Two differences. It is a *move* of
one running clock, never a second one minted alongside the children's.
And it exists to catch time no subtask claims — a problem this design
already answers elsewhere, since every interval is attributed to the
heading a session named and unattributed guideposts have
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]].

*Proposed rule, narrower than "never clock a parent."* Parent clocking
should be *deliberate, never automatic*. The epic's existing =1:08= is
real epic-level work — planning on 08-06, revising the plan against what
shipped on 08-11 — and a blanket "only clock leaves" would discard that
category. So: exempt container headings from the auto-clock-in trigger,
and leave manual =org_clock_in= on a parent available for genuine
coordination work.

*Detecting a container, org-natively:* norang's lazy definition — a
heading with at least one child carrying a TODO keyword. No new
property, no list to maintain.

*Consequence for CLAUDE.md:* "any transition *to* =DOING= or =PLANNING=
must open a clock" acquires a stated exception, which belongs with
[[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]]'s rewrite rather
than as a separate edit.

*Two things observed while diagnosing this, both worth their own
attention and neither fixed here:*

1. *Deleting the open CLOCK line desynced the live clock* —
   =org-clock-marker= slid onto the *next*, already-closed line
   (=[2026-08-11 Tue 11:10]--[11:13] =>  0:03=), while =org-clocking-p=
   stayed =t=. Any subsequent =org-clock-out= — Emacs quit and suspend
   both run one — would have closed a clock at that marker and corrupted
   a good interval. Note =org-clock-cancel= is *not* the recovery: it
   deletes a region relative to that same wrong marker. Cleared by
   resetting =org-clock-marker=/=org-clock-hd-marker= in place, touching
   no file. This is
   [[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][53b0047d]] observed live,
   and evidence it is not merely theoretical.
2. *=org-log-into-drawer= is nil*, so org's native state-change logging
   lands *outside* =:LOGBOOK:= — visible on b5f7c5c7, whose =DOING= note
   sits above the drawer while older entries sit inside it. Applied
   transitions do land inside, so the two paths disagree. Relevant
   because native logging working correctly is the entire premise of the
   apply design.

   *Resolved 2026-08-12, elsewhere.* Fixed by adding =#+STARTUP:
   logdrawer= to =TODO.org= and =DONE.org=, which sets the variable
   buffer-locally and leaves a user's other org files alone. The
   documentation half belongs to
   [[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]], with drift #4,
   since it is the same header block; the "what may a module change on
   someone else's machine" question it opened is
   [[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]]. Left recorded
   here because this is where it was found, not because this heading
   owns it.

*A third consequence, found 2026-08-14 while designing something else.*
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] has been =DOING=
since =[2026-08-06 Thu 22:35]= — eight days — with no clock running. In
that whole time *no session-start report has ever mentioned it*, because
=claude-code-ide-org--wait-headings-context= reports only the currently
clocked heading plus =WAIT= headings.

This surfaced while considering adding =DOING= to that report's keyword set, so
a new session would be told about headings left mid-flight. Simulated against
the real files, the proposal produced four lines where today there is one — and
three of the four are currently invisible: two leaves abandoned that morning
([[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]] and [[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]]), and this epic, which would then appear in *every*
session-start report, unchanged, indefinitely.

*The finding is that the two are the same keyword and opposite news.* A
container in =DOING= is a true and unremarkable statement about the
project. A leaf in =DOING= with no clock is an increment somebody walked
away from. Only the second is worth telling a new session, and a report
that always carries the same line stops being read at all — the same
failure that
[[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]]'s outcome note
records for evidence lines, which were applied without evaluation
precisely because they were always plausible.

*So the report change is downstream of this heading, not independent of
it.* If containers stopped carrying a work-in-progress keyword — or if
the container test proposed above existed anywhere reusable — the report
would need no filter at all: adding =DOING= to its keyword set would
simply do the right thing. Building the filter first would work around
the problem this heading exists to remove, and would leave two
definitions of "container" to keep in step, which is the shape of bug
this project keeps finding (see the six-keyword denylist retired from
=--review-suggest-heading= on 2026-08-14 for the same reason).

*Not the earlier symptom recurring.* The epic's clock is *closed*; this
is not the auto-clock-in trigger minting an unwanted interval again. It
is the same root — a container carrying a keyword that asserts work is
in progress — showing a second, quieter symptom eight days later. The
first cost an interval that had to be deleted by hand; this one costs
nothing visible, which is why it went unnoticed for eight days.

*And it does not affect span attribution,* which is worth stating so a
future reader does not assume it does:
=claude-code-ide-org--review-suggest-heading= derives its guess from
queued =todo= events, never from the state on disk, so an epic sitting
in =DOING= in the file is not a latch target.

*Scale, measured 2026-08-14:* 37 direct children, 10 of them still open.
The "ten children" in the clocktable measurement above was that day's
count; ten is now the number that are unfinished.

*Outcome, 2026-08-14.* Shipped as proposed, with no design changes.
=claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= implements norang's lazy
definition directly — scan the subtree, non-nil on the first descendant
carrying a TODO keyword — and
=claude-code-ide-org--trigger-auto-clock-in= gains it as a third guard
beside the existing re-entrancy and already-clocked-here checks. No new
property, no list to maintain, and the exemption covers =PLANNING= as
well as =DOING= since both run through that one trigger.

*Verified.* Five new tests (248 total, all green), each confirmed to
discriminate by mutating the source in two directions rather than by
passing once: forcing the guard to =t= failed exactly the two skip tests
and left the "still clocks a heading with a plain child" test passing;
deleting the keyword check from the predicate inverted that precisely —
the two skip tests passed while the predicate test and the plain-child
test failed. Then reloaded into the live Emacs and run against the real
files, where the predicate answers =t= for
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] and =nil= for both
[[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]] and this heading.

*One thing the heading assumed that is no longer true, and it narrows
the fix.* =--review-apply-item= binds
=claude-code-ide-org--auto-clock-in-active= for its whole duration, so
apply never reaches this trigger at all. A hand-edit in Emacs is now the
*only* path that does. That does not weaken the fix — the 2026-08-12
incident that prompted this heading was a hand-toggle, which is exactly
the surviving case — but "fires on *any* transition" above should be
read as "any transition outside apply."

=CLAUDE.md= deliberately untouched: this heading routes that edit
through [[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]]'s rewrite,
which already records the exception in its own scope notes.

*What this unblocks, and it is the obvious next move rather than part of
this heading:* the container test now exists somewhere reusable, which
is the precondition the "report change is downstream" section above set.
Adding =DOING= to =--wait-headings-context='s keyword set and filtering
containers through this predicate would now do the right thing, with one
definition of "container" rather than two.

:PROPERTIES:

CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 10:48]--[2026-08-14 Fri 10:49] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-14 Fri 10:48]--[2026-08-14 Fri 10:49] suggested span, accepted at review
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-14 Fri 11:56]
:END:

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
on 2026-08-13.* A capability that epic *enables* rather than one it
requires — the queue and review loop are complete without it. Filed
there only because that is where the idea surfaced, which is proximity
rather than membership.

*Prototyped by hand before being proposed, 2026-08-13*, which is why it
is filed with a recommendation rather than a question. Asked how to
identify what an unattributed span was circling, the answer that worked
was neither a transcript nor a model: *git commits and =:CREATED:=
stamps falling inside the span's own window.* Three shell commands
resolved three of four spans unambiguously.

The two strongest signals, and they differ:
- A =:CREATED:= stamp *inside* the span is the best evidence there is —
  a heading written during that time names what was being thought about.
  The 15:30--15:34 span brackets a heading created 15:33.
- A commit *just after* the span is next best, since work precedes its
  commit. That same span is followed by a commit at 15:34.

Note the asymmetry: a commit timestamp marks when work was *finished*,
not when it happened, so it anchors a span rather than bounding one. A
4-minute span preceded its commit by seconds; a 23-minute span contained
three.

*The proposal:* render each unassigned span in the review buffer
alongside the commits and headings created in its window. Nothing more —
the human still decides. Assignment stops requiring a separate =git log=
by hand, which is what it required 23 times in one sitting.

*Why this rather than
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]], and why it is that
heading's sibling rather than its child.* =0c8644ff= is specifically
"ask Claude", and carries three transport blockers plus an anchoring
objection: a fluent recommendation is harder to reject than a
transparently mechanical one, which erodes the safety property review
exists to provide. This proposal inherits none of it. No sampling, so
the transport question is moot; no transcript, so that scope question
does not arise; and it presents *evidence* rather than a recommendation,
so there is nothing to anchor on.

It is therefore not a part of =0c8644ff= but the thing to build instead,
and first — building it is what narrows =0c8644ff= to its actual
residue: spans straddling two tasks, where commits exist on both sides
and only a narrative says where the boundary fell.

*A structural reason for sibling too, worth stating because it is a
convention rather than an accident:* =TODO.org= has exactly three
heading levels — category, task, epic-child — and *zero* level-4
headings across 99 of them. A child here would introduce a fourth level
for one heading.

*Scope notes for whoever builds it.* The git query wants the repository
containing the tracked files, not =default-directory=, which at review
time is wherever the human happens to be. Heading creations are cheap to
find without scanning — =:CREATED:= is a property, so =org-map-entries=
with a property match does it. And the window should probably extend a
few minutes past the span's end to catch the commit that concluded it,
which is the asymmetry noted above.

*Outcome, 2026-08-13/14.* Shipped: an unassigned span in the review
buffer renders the git commits and =:CREATED:= stamps falling inside its
own window, interleaved chronologically. The window extends past the
span's end but never before its start, and the git query runs against the
repository containing the tracked files, found by truename so the
=~/org= symlink resolves. Memoized per window, since the render runs on
every mark keystroke. 9 ERT tests, discrimination checked by reverting
the render hook and the error guard separately.

*First real use, 2026-08-14, and it falsified the premise the feature was
built on.* Asked whether the evidence helped, the user answered: "frankly
I did not evaluate critically, everything looked reasonable." The batch
was applied without the lines being weighed.

That is exactly the anchoring objection recorded against
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] — a fluent
recommendation is harder to reject than a transparently mechanical one —
arriving by a route this heading explicitly claimed to escape. The
argument above for building this *instead* of =0c8644ff= was that it
"presents *evidence* rather than a recommendation, so there is nothing to
anchor on." Tried once, that claim did not hold. Supporting detail makes
a suggestion easier to accept whether or not it is phrased as a
recommendation.

*A second finding, about how the report itself arrived.* The first
version of the feedback was that the commit lines needed timestamps —
which they already had, in the first column. Rendering the actual output
and putting it in front of the user resolved it: "I see them now; I may
have misremembered." Worth recording because acting on the literal
request would have added timestamps that already existed and left the
real problem untouched. Reconstructing the artifact rather than trusting
either party's recollection is what made the difference, which is the
same lesson as the standing rule about preferring the primary artifact.

*The response: report absences, not only support.* Stretches of a span
with no evidence in them are now rendered as gaps, above
=claude-code-ide-org-span-evidence-gap= (default 5 minutes, measured
against the span and never the slack window). The intent is rhetorical
rather than informational — a commit list says "here is support for the
suggestion", while a gap says "here is what you still have to decide",
which is the posture review is supposed to have.

The case that matters most is the empty one. A span with no evidence used
to render *no lines at all*, making "nothing was found here" visually
identical to "nobody looked". It now reads
=(nothing for 40m, 02:00-02:40)=. Verified live on 2026-08-14 against a
real span and a fabricated overnight one; 4 further tests, each confirmed
to fail by mutation (gaps suppressed, gaps measured against the slack
window instead of the span, threshold ignored).

*Deliberately not claimed: that this fixes it.* The previous claim of
exactly this shape — that a particular presentation would keep review
critical — was falsified on first contact. Whether gap lines actually
induce evaluation is unknown until the next batch is reviewed, and the
honest test is to ask again afterwards rather than to assume.

[[file:~/.claude/plans/sessions-logbook-ordering.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-04 from conversation. Org's own =:LOGBOOK:= (both
=CLOCK:= lines and state-change log notes) inserts each new entry at
the *top* of the drawer, newest-first — visible throughout this file,
e.g. the =PLANNING TODO state= heading in "Skill logic" above, whose
=CLOCK:= lines run 17:29, 17:15, 14:55, 14:45, descending. This
project's own =:SESSIONS:= drawer
(=claude-code-ide-org-session-pause=/=-resume=) instead appends each
new =Resumed=/=Paused= entry at the *bottom*, oldest-first — the
reverse order, visible in the same headings' =:SESSIONS:= drawers.

Adjust =:SESSIONS:= to match — assuming, as this task's title takes
for granted, that matching =:LOGBOOK:='s (and the native TODO
state-change log's) ordering convention is actually the right call
rather than an arbitrary pick between two equally-valid options; that
assumption itself is worth a sanity check during planning, not just
asserted here. If confirmed, likely means prepending new
=:SESSIONS:= entries instead of appending, in
=claude-code-ide-org-session-pause=/=-resume=.

Distinct from the existing "Known cosmetic quirk" in CLAUDE.md about
=:SESSIONS:= vs =:LOGBOOK:='s *relative drawer position* (which drawer
appears first in the property area) — this is about the entry order
*within* =:SESSIONS:= itself.

Shares code surface with
[[id:d150c02e-06fe-4514-bf69-e2cc5d8df4c7][org_clock_in doesn't record clock ownership, unlike session-resume]]
— both would touch =claude-code-ide-org--log-session-event= and/or
the =session-pause=/=-resume= internals. Not a blocking order, just
worth checking the other's status before starting either.

*Cancelled 2026-08-12 by
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]] — and this
reverses that heading's own stated prior.* Both =e51d6ba1= ("=2b0988ed=
looks likeliest to survive either way") and the epic's plan-staleness
table ("most likely still applies independent of this refactor")
predate the decision at
[[id:9d2fcdad-9bf7-47b6-8018-223b13ec4577][9d2fcdad]] to delete the
=:SESSIONS:= drawers outright rather than leave them inert (commits
=039f547=, =c38a497=). Both were reasonable when written; shipped code
falsified them. There is no drawer left to reorder and no
=session-pause=/=-resume= left to prepend in — =config.el='s tombstone
comment stands where they did. The transferable question, what ordering
the *surviving* drawer should use, was answered separately and shipped
by [[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]] (commit
=4d2dcab=): one *ascending* timeline across every entry style — the
opposite of the newest-first convention this heading proposed matching.


* CANCELLED session-resume blindly inherits any session's last-clocked heading :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 17:37]
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       582cc7f4-41a2-4666-ad3f-1b76b459147e
:CREATED:  [2026-08-04 Tue 18:27]
:ARCHIVE_TIME: 2026-08-17 Mon 17:13
:ARCHIVE_FILE: ~/git/claude-code-ide-org/TODO.org
:ARCHIVE_OLPATH: Clock lifecycle & visibility
:ARCHIVE_CATEGORY: TODO
:ARCHIVE_TODO: CANCELLED
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "CANCELLED"  from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-12 Wed 17:37] \\
  superseded by feba67eb's cutover; see e51d6ba1
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/session-resume-continuity-guard.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-04, observed live and confirmed with real evidence,
not just a one-off guess: immediately after deliberately clocking out
(via the sibling heading above's manual recovery, with no task
actually being worked on — this conversation was pure "care and
feeding" of the project plan, not implementation work), the very next
prompt silently clocked back in on the unrelated =PLANNING TODO state=
heading (ID b95b9fba-f78e-48fe-8546-988709cce309). Root cause:
=session-resume= (the =UserPromptSubmit= hook) unconditionally calls
=org-clock-in-last=, which resumes whichever heading is most recent in
the *global, shared* =org-clock-history= — with no check on whether
*this session* is the one that put it there.

This is broader than "no task in view this turn," and confirmed to
also bite genuinely concurrent sessions, not just idle/maintenance
ones: a =ps= check during this same conversation found two real
=claude= CLI processes running concurrently (separate terminals), and
the =PLANNING TODO state= heading's own =:SESSIONS:= history showed a
*different* session ID pausing/resuming its clock at one point — not
because that session was doing =PLANNING=-state work (confirmed:
=b95b9fba= appears nowhere in =config.el=, so no implementation code
for that feature has landed from *any* session), but almost certainly
that session's own ordinary turn-boundary hook grabbing the same
shared global clock via the same blind =org-clock-in-last= call.
Because the clock is one global resource, *any* session's resume can
silently inherit and misattribute time to whatever heading happens to
sit at the head of =org-clock-history=, regardless of who set it
=DOING= or whether anyone is actually touching it. The existing
session-id-ownership fix (see "Concurrent Claude Code sessions share
one global org-clock", ID 337f7fb2-b9e9-4c02-82dd-d88e60df364b) only
guards a clock that's *actively running* from being paused/stolen by a
different session mid-flight — it does nothing to stop an *idle*
clock from being resumed onto the wrong heading by whichever session's
=UserPromptSubmit= happens to fire next.

Concrete fix direction (sharper than originally captured): make
=session-resume= conditional on session continuity — only call
=org-clock-in-last= if the clock's most recent close (the last
=:SESSIONS:= =Paused= entry, or the session-id already recorded by the
337f7fb2 work) belongs to *this same session's* ID; otherwise leave it
clocked out and require an explicit =org_clock_in= once real work
actually starts on some heading.

Related, same "single shared global clock" theme, distinct root
causes — worth planning together but not assuming they're one fix:
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][Direct file writes to a live org buffer can desync org-clock-marker]]
(external writes corrupting Emacs's in-memory marker) and
[[id:d150c02e-06fe-4514-bf69-e2cc5d8df4c7][org_clock_in doesn't record clock ownership, unlike session-resume]]
(most clock-start paths never record an owner in the first place, so
this bug's own "idle clock" precondition is the common case, not the
exception).

*Cancelled 2026-08-12 by
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]].* Superseded by
shipped work, not deferred. =bin/hooks/session-resume= no longer calls
=org-clock-in-last=, or reaches Emacs at all: it appends a bare =resume=
guidepost to the session's own queue file and exits. A guidepost names
no heading, so there is no wrong heading left to inherit — attribution
comes from the surrounding =clock_in=/=clock_out= events at review time.
=claude-code-ide-org-session-pause=/=-resume= were deleted at the
2026-08-11 cutover ([[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]],
commit =220ba78=); the hook script's own header names this heading as
the bug it removed. The failure mode documented here remains the
clearest statement of why that cutover was worth doing.

  ownership variables deleted at cutover; see e51d6ba1

[[file:~/.claude/plans/clock-in-ownership-recording.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-05, hit live, then re-confirmed and sharpened
2026-08-05 with a direct empirical check. Original finding: manually
clocking a heading in via the =org_clock_in= MCP tool
(=claude-code-ide-org-clock-in=) logs a plain "Resumed" =:SESSIONS:=
entry with no session-id, and never sets
=claude-code-ide-org--clock-owner-session-id=, unlike
=claude-code-ide-org-session-resume=.

*Actual scope, confirmed by exhaustively grepping every call site*:
=(org-clock-in)= is only ever invoked from two places —
=claude-code-ide-org-clock-in= (line ~362) and
=claude-code-ide-org--trigger-auto-clock-in= (line ~1596, fired on
*any* =DOING=/=PLANNING= transition via =org-trigger-hook=, so also
on every =org_set_todo= call and every hand-edit). Meanwhile
=claude-code-ide-org--clock-owner-session-id= is only ever *set* from
one place: =claude-code-ide-org-session-resume=. So owner-recording
covers exactly one of the three ways a clock can start — the
*resume-after-pause* case — while the more common case (transitioning
a heading straight to =DOING=/=PLANNING=) is unowned by construction.
Checked the =337f7fb2= DONE writeup: it only ever describes threading
=session_id= through =session-pause=/=-resume=, never claims trigger-
hook coverage — so this is an honest original scope gap, not a
regression.

*Live-verified, not just theorized*: while another session had a
genuine =PLANNING= heading actively clocked (ID
4cda6bf7-441b-47b2-b3d5-b8c7e7a207a5, set via =org_set_todo=, the
"proper" path), a direct =emacsclient= query showed
=claude-code-ide-org--clock-owner-session-id= =nil= while
=claude-code-ide-org--planning-owner-session-id= correctly held that
session's ID — same heading, same instant, one guard fully engaged,
the other completely absent. =337f7fb2='s own guard is deliberately
permissive when no owner is recorded ("the correct fallback for the
ordinary single-session case, not merely a stopgap," per its own
docstring) — so this isn't broken protection, it's protection that
was never wired to the common code path.

Sharper fix direction than originally captured: the precedent already
exists in this exact codebase.
=claude-code-ide-org--maybe-record-planning-owner= (a =PostToolUse=
hook matched on =org_set_todo=, reading =session_id= straight from its
own hook payload) already solves the identical problem for
=planning-owner-session-id=. The same hook — or a sibling of it — can
set =clock-owner-session-id= too, whenever the requested state is
=DOING=/=PLANNING=, without needing to instrument the trigger hook or
=org_clock_in= itself. =org_clock_in='s own missing session-id is a
narrower, separate case (no =PostToolUse= payload carries a =state=
field for a bare clock-in call) worth a smaller, second fix alongside
it.

Related, same "single shared global clock" theme:
[[id:582cc7f4-41a2-4666-ad3f-1b76b459147e][session-resume blindly inherits any session's last-clocked heading]]
and
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][Direct file writes to a live org buffer can desync org-clock-marker]]
— three distinct root causes in the same subsystem, worth planning
together, not assumed to be one fix.

*Cancelled 2026-08-12 by
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]].* Both halves are
structurally gone rather than fixed. =--clock-owner-session-id= and
=--planning-owner-session-id= were deleted at the 2026-08-11 cutover
([[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]], commit
=220ba78=) — they survive only inside =config.el='s tombstone comment —
along with =--maybe-record-planning-owner=, the =PostToolUse= hook this
heading proposed reusing. =org_clock_in= now validates an =:ID:= and
opens no clock, so there is no ownership left to record.

The want behind the heading is better served than the fix it proposed:
=bin/hooks/queue-append= stamps =session_id=, =agent_id=, =agent_type=
and =source= on *every* event, and the queue is keyed per session by
filename — so attribution is recorded per event and survives an Emacs
restart, which no global variable did. Caveat worth carrying forward:
as of 2026-08-12 the =agent_id=/=agent_type= fields have never been
populated in 347 events, so that half is designed but unexercised.


* CANCELLED Audit for clock/session bookkeeping noise before committing during a clocking-suppressed session :code:
CLOSED: [2026-08-12 Wed 17:37]
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       782cda6c-111c-454e-93eb-b2b27012078b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-05 Wed 14:28]
:ARCHIVE_TIME: 2026-08-17 Mon 17:13
:ARCHIVE_FILE: ~/git/claude-code-ide-org/TODO.org
:ARCHIVE_OLPATH: Clock lifecycle & visibility
:ARCHIVE_CATEGORY: TODO
:ARCHIVE_TODO: CANCELLED
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "CANCELLED"  from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-12 Wed 17:37] \\
  folded into the review UI; see e51d6ba1
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/bookkeeping-noise-audit.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-05, raised in conversation: when a session has been
explicitly told to suppress its own clocking for a while (as this one
was), other concurrent sessions' ordinary activity can still write
real =:LOGBOOK:=/=:SESSIONS:= deltas into headings this session
touches or observes — visible throughout this same session (the
=PLANNING TODO state= heading and the notes.org review-task heading
both picked up cross-session clock churn while under the
suppression). The open question: at commit time, should a
clocking-suppressed session specifically double-check that whatever
clock/session bookkeeping deltas are about to be committed are
*genuine* (real, attributable work — its own or another session's)
rather than side-effect noise the suppressed session itself
incidentally observed or triggered, before folding them into a commit
under this session's authorship?

Not yet clear whether this needs new tooling (e.g. a pre-commit diff
review step specific to =:LOGBOOK:=/=:SESSIONS:= hunks) or is purely a
standing-instruction-level judgment call for whoever is about to
commit — same register as other "only the model/user can decide"
rules already in CLAUDE.md. Related to, but broader than, the sibling
heading above (that one is about a specific missing ownership
mechanism; this one is about what to do with the resulting mess at
commit time, regardless of whether that mechanism ever gets fixed).

Also substantial overlap with
[[id:3cb3f955-f10a-47cd-84ab-e629d73ea59d][Org skill: commit a newly-captured TODO immediately, when safe]] —
that heading already raised the same underlying question in the
abstract before this one found a concrete incident. See its own
2026-08-05 update.

*Second concrete incident, 2026-08-06 — a lost interval, not observed
noise, and a root cause none of the recorded incidents cover.* Found by
running exactly the pre-commit audit this heading proposes, during the
Phase 0 verification for
[[id:3d576d29-747f-40b9-a5b3-ecc76679daad][3d576d29]]. Sequence: clocked
in on =3d576d29= at 22:35; a live =emacsclient= probe set =DOING= on a
scratch heading, which fired
=claude-code-ide-org--trigger-auto-clock-in= and moved the clock into
the scratch buffer; recovered with =org-clock-cancel=, then re-clocked
=3d576d29= at ~22:43 and clocked out at 22:45. Both =org_clock_in= and
=org_clock_out= reported success. The heading ends up with:

: CLOCK: [2026-08-06 Thu 22:35]--[2026-08-06 Thu 22:40] =>  0:05

— and *no* second interval for 22:43-22:45. =:SESSIONS:= brackets the
full arc correctly (=Resumed 22:37= / =Paused 22:45=); only the CLOCK
interval vanished. Roughly two minutes, so materially irrelevant; the
point is the failure mode, not the magnitude.

*Root cause found 2026-08-07, correcting the paragraph above:* nothing
to do with =org-clock-cancel=, and not a bug — it is documented,
deliberate behavior. =claude-code-ide-org-clock-out= calls
=claude-code-ide-org-consolidate-history= on every clock-out, which
rounds each closed interval to the nearest 5 minutes
(=claude-code-ide-org--round-time-to-5-minutes=, config.el:900; ties
round *up*) and then drops anything that becomes zero-length
(=--consolidate-logbook-text=, config.el:991, mirroring org's own
=org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= intent after rounding). 22:43
rounds to 22:45; 22:45 rounds to 22:45; zero; dropped. The earlier
interval survived only because 22:35→22:41 rounded to 22:35→22:40.

Generalized, the rule is: *any interval shorter than about 2.5 minutes
that does not straddle a 5-minute boundary disappears silently on
clock-out.* That is a much broader exposure than one incident — it
applies to every short clock the tools have ever closed. Whether the
5-minute granularity is still the right trade is now a live question
rather than a settled one, and it is decided for the new architecture
in [[id:32272061-1d78-4726-b13b-90338edb2ba5][32272061]]'s plan: apply
writes exactly the endpoints the human confirms, with no rounding and
no zero-drop.

What still makes it worth recording separately: every other incident
catalogued here is about *cross-session* interference or a desynced
marker. This one is single-session, sequential, and purely a
consequence of this project's own consolidation policy — no
concurrency involved at all. Deliberately not "fixed" by
hand-writing the missing line: CLAUDE.md forbids editing CLOCK entries
directly, and fabricating an interval to make the record look tidy is
the exact "confidently wrong" record
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] exists to
prevent.

Note the incident is also mild evidence *for* this heading's premise:
the audit caught it, and it would otherwise have been committed
unnoticed inside a 268-line documentation diff.

*Cancelled 2026-08-12 by
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]], along the line
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] predicted:* its
mechanism-approach "likely folds into the new review UI rather than
standing alone." All three parts of the premise dissolved. "A
clocking-suppressed session" has no referent — no session clocks at all
since the 2026-08-11 cutover. =:SESSIONS:= no longer exists, and the
only automatic =:LOGBOOK:= writers left fire from =org-todo=, which now
runs only inside the human-driven apply or a human's own hand-edit. And
the genuine-vs-noise judgment is now made *before* anything lands, in
the review buffer with session and timing visible, which is what this
proposed pre-commit audit was reconstructing after the fact.

The second incident recorded above (the vanished 2-minute interval) was
root-caused separately: =consolidate-history='s 5-minute rounding,
retired in =f0fc4a2=. Apply writes exact confirmed endpoints with
=org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= bound nil.

The surviving commit-hygiene question is not lost —
[[id:3cb3f955-f10a-47cd-84ab-e629d73ea59d][3cb3f955]] holds the
single-session half and
[[id:2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c][2aeb65d6]] the cross-session
one, which found the real hazard to be the shared =.git/index=, not
drawer deltas: a different mechanism from the one hypothesised here.
